KILBURN then moved : "That this meeting especially protests against contracting nut and will never be satisfied with any arrangement which fails (1), to include our schools in the national system ; (2), to safeguard at the same time their complete Catholic character ; (3) to place them on terms of absolute equality with the provided scheole of the country." The 360,000 Catholic school children, he said, were told by the Government in a cold-blooded manner that no place could be found for them in the national system, that all the educational advantages which they now enjoyed equally with their playmates must be given up and they must be satisfied nail inferior buildings, less qualified teachers, and the scantiest apparatus. There was no doubt that before the Bill went through the Mouse of Commons a bigger bribe would he offered to them, but he hoped they mould have no hesitation in rejecting it with scorn. They wonld never be satisfied with any arrangement that failed to safeguard the Catholic character of their schools, and by that they meant devotion to the Mass and Sacraments, respect to

ior; tnen aaa tiny pinch at linety- powdered sweet herbs and as much minced garlic as will go ,on the point of a very small knife. Draw the casserole to the side of the fro, add a bouquet garni, ..and simmer very slowly until the fowl . is thoroughly cooked. The time this will take varies according to the Gge of the bird in question.' . Forty minutes should suffice for an ordinary chicken, while' for a tough old fowl an hour And a - half, and in some cases even two hours, will not be too much.. Baste every few minutes, and when done dish up on a hot dish and garnish with the bacon. Remove the bayleaf, parsley, &c., and pour away all the remaining ,grease. Add to the residue of the butter from half a gill to a gill of well-flavoured brown stock or gravy, a tablespoonful of sherry, pepper and salt to taste, and a tiny squeeze of lemon juice. Boil up sharply, and let it continue boiling until it thickens. Then pour over the fowl and serve at once, accompanied by straw. or chipped potatoes. It is worthy of note that for this dish a soup—that is, an old fowl—will do just as well as a spring chicken, ani, if cooked. carefully and slowly, it is prsetically impossible to tell the difference.

VAGARIES OF THE PLUMB LINE. One of the curious things that men of science have discovered in their innumerable efforts to measure and map the earth with the least possible error is the fact that there are places where the direction of a plumb line is not vertical. Irregularities of density in the crust of the globe may produce this phenome- non. A remarkable instance has been found in the island of Porto Rico, where the devia- tion from the vertical is so great that, in map- ping the island, the northern and southern coast lines, as shewn on the older maps, had each to be moved inward half a mile.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

A VERY ELEGANT COAT.

Pictured. in the accompanying illustration is a particularly modish and attractive long coat of the elegant type so much in vogue this winter. This beautiful garment is, of course, only suitable for a smart occasion, such as a call, .an afternoon reception, or an afternoon concert, aic. For such it is extremely .smart. The original was carried out in the finest soft cloth, of medium weight and very glossy sur- face, and in a charming tone of mole, a tone that has more brown than grey in it. The side pieces of the coat were cut rather shorter than the back and front, and were arranged in a becoming and original fashion. The top part was draped over the shoulders, and then allowed to bang quite straight to the hem, being caught slightly to the figure a* short

ki'

waist length with a scroll strap, braided with soutache of the same shade. This strnp merely passed under the arms and finished on ; either side of the back and front. This back and front- were formed by long, plain stole pieces of the cloth placed underneath the plain sides, and cut a few inches longer at the • bottom. These pieces were completely covered with 'a handsome design worked in , soutache Ai,nd embroidery silk cf the shade of • the cloth. The sleeves were quite small, of - full length, and were braided to match the • front and back.

THE WIDTH OF SKIRTS. Three yards is the utmost width allowed to the modish skirt about the hem. This forbids the wearer to take- long steps or to make any sudden, unpremeditated move- ment. The knees have to be kept close to- gether and the feet must be near companions in order to mai4tsin the. OXigIIOU3 line of the narrow shirt. These conditions are not appropriate to busy lives, remarks a Wo3 writer, but answer as well as anything ells for a novelty to those, who live to dress. Un- fortunately, however, but few English figures look well in these very clinging skirts and short-waisted bodices. English' figures are as good as any in the world, but the English car- riage leaves much to be desired. In child- hood and girlhood lounging and stooping are L i too rauei allowed, just as they are with our boys in public schools. Fencing is the proper ti cure for this defect. Fencing tenches grace ' without stiffness, whereas drill fails in the I latter particolar. ; I A PRETTY AND SIMPLE PROCK.

In blue, dark green, brown or red serge, or cashmere, it would make an admirable regu- lar,- wear rbursery frock; whilst tarried .out in thick white washing satin, white Jap silk, silk cashmere, 'white cloth, or . white ce:pe-de- chine, it would be simply charming for a party, and would have a chic• all its own. •The little overdress is perfectly plain, and is the same • shape both back., and front. At the top it is slightly shaped, and is finished " with a piping or tubing of satin, a heavy cord, or merely stitching. Back and front are con- nected by two straps over the shoulders on either side. These straps are edged to match the top, and are caught down with pretty but- tons. TO-DAY'S RECIPE. FOWL EN CASSEHOLE.—The great advan- tage, of casserole cookery is that by its aid teugh game or fouls,. may be rendered deli- cately tender and appetising. Here is a recipe which, on the authority of the DailAT Crapitic, can be applied with equal success to either an old fowl or pheasant or a brace of last year's partridges, and the resuliS, if the directions liven are carefully followed, will be bound to be good. - Take a fowl, trussed, as if for boiling; dust it lightly with spiced Eiffel Tower: pepper, and reserve. Place two ounces of butter or clarified beef 'dripping in a clean fireproof china casserole, and as soon as it boils add the fowl or. game, together with four ounces of streaky bacon freed from rind, A ed. paCket makes 15 delicious light buns with ece. and cut into neat strips. PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

THE QUEEN'S GIFT BOOK. This excellent outcome of the work of the camera of the Queen is now on sale, and the publishers say that it is likely to reach a record sale of something over a million copies, which will be far beyond anything attained by any picture gift book -up to the present. One might suppose that so many snapshots without letterpress wculd he somewhat tame and dull, but the work is so good,and the variety of incident and treatment so great, that the interest is maintained from the first page to the last very fully. As the cost of the book is only 2s. 6d.,and more than ls.w ill go to charity for every copy that is sold, none of my readers can do much better than include the hook in his Christmas present

===== 0002601_19081205_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
SNAP-SHOOTING.

The publication of the Queen's Book has probably led to a greater use of the word " snap-shot" and its derivatives than has ever been made at one time. It seems worth while, therefore, to protest against the ugly and incorrect words "snap-abetting," " snap-shotter,' and " stap-shotted." If photographing is to be described as shooting (whether snap or otherwise), it is only right and seemly that the verb" to shoot" should Fe conjugated in the regular manner, and that we should speak of snap-shooting "and "snap-shooters.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCOTTISH SALON.

This exhibition, for which entries close on the 7th December, promises to be quite up to the high standard reached by its forerunners. The foreign Loan Collec- tion will be of the works of Dr. E. G. Boon, whose delightful and absolutely "straight "photography has I believe, never been seen in a really representative collection. English work will be represented by a number of landscapes of tbe late Mr.A. Horsley Hinton whose death was tragically connected with the last Scottish Salon, as be returned from the opening straight to what proved his fatal illness. 'HIGH-POWER LIME LIGHT.

Mr. C. E. S. Phillips, who is one of cur most ingenious and successful experimenters in physics and electricity, makes a very practical suggestion in a recent issue of" Nature " for increasing the power of the oxyhydrogen lime light. His method consists of placing over the lime an ordinary incandescent mantle. He finds that this increases the power of the light tremendously, and that so long as the lime is burned regularly the mantle will stand through a whole evening's display,

===== 0002601_19081205_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TFIALES COLOUR PLATE,-

I have more than once mentioned this British-made plate for photography in natural colours, in this column. It is now actually on the market, and has several points which will recommend it very strongly to all who wish to make natural -colour pictures. Perhaps the greatest point is one which has not been particularly emphasised, so for as I know, tamely, that is the light-filter is supplied seperate from the plate, it will be possible from one exposure to make any number of colour- positives without any difficulty. The cheapness of the plate is an advantage, and this comes in bcth directly and indirectly, for the price of four quarter-plates with two light-filters, is 2s. 6d., and if there should be a failure In any particular exposure or development, the loss only involves the canchromatic plate, which can be replaced at the rate of about 38. per dozen, the colour- screen plate being available for use again. Generally speaking, the operations are somewhat similar to those with the autochrome plate, excepting that, RS already stated, the light-filter is separate, and also that the panchromatic plate used is gelatine-coated, and can be treated just as any ordinary panchromatic plate without special precautions of any kind.

SEEING THINGS BEFORE THEY HAPPEN. A 'lecture by Mr. Adolph Abrahams at The Royal Photographic Society lately was full of good things, but one of the most interesting points was his demon- stration of the fact that the photographer viho did much focal plane work .developed a habit of seeing things slightly before they happened. with the result that could Frees the bulb of his shutter and catch actions at the quickest focal plane exposures exactly at the point be desired, although, of couse, if he waited until the actions actually happened, he would be a little too late, as an appreciable, thougb short, space of time is occupied in conveying the impulse from the eye to the brain and from the brain to the bulb. A curious thing about this is that the photographer does not realise that he is pressing the bulb before the- critical me went, tut thints that he sees the incident before pressing.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
PERSONALITY IN LANDSCAPE WORE.,

A writer in the December issue of "The Photographic Monthly " who illustrates his subject with landscapes by Mr. A. Slatter and Mt. Ward Muir, points out how even in perfectly simple landscapes the individuality of the photographer is apparent at every point. For the purposes of comparison he imagines two men of different temperaments photographing the same view, and says t-- " One man, going forth on a winter morning, with nerves a-tingle end the blood coursing wartrilf,through his arteries, may see only the snap and vigour of the frost ; while another man, poetically mooded and con- templative notices only the softness of contour, the beauty of fight and shade, and the soft shadows caused by the ample reflecting power of the snow. If both are , photographing, each will scel; the scene, the arrange- ment of lines and tones, that fits the mood. And each will give the exposure, the development, the printing, mounting and framing that emphasises his impression ,of the subject. That is, if"trach man is a picture-maker. The crisp-visioned man wilrlook for n. scene with the '1 sun-shadows thrown toward him, giving crispness and nrinf rast illneiect a composition of few elements, with strong, simple lines ; will probably focus sharply, giving an exposure somewhat "under" rather than over, land will develop and print fur "pluck." He will mount, lend frame in tones that contrast distinctly. The softer mocded man will prefer the day with slight haze ' • the time when the thaw has just been sufficient to shroud the sunshine slightly end to mate distant objects veily grey. He will choose a composition with many balanc- ing lines and messes, without harsh contrasts ; will expose fully, develop and print for softness ; arid mount and frame quite delicately, witba series of broad grey tones of paper and (probably) a passe-partott I finish, just hound with a thin grey line. Individuality is shown at every step from the first " seeing " of the view, to the framing, with the heavy 1 close-up, black-and-brown moulding in the one case,and the narrow paper binding beyond wide mounts in the other.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE END OF THE WORLD.

As to the length of time the earth is likely to last, the calculations are that it will not cease to be active for a good many millions of years, such activity not, however, necessarily supposing that life as we know it now will always be possible, the eventuality of a uni- versal ice age being always a contingency that may occur again in the history of the globe. It is intere3ting to note that in this connec- tion a Swedish mystic called Stromberg has declared that the world would never know another ice, age, but that it was now running out its course to the end. Its existence, he declared, would endure as long as fire burnt in the earth's interior—that is, until the whole mass shall have become solidified. As the process of cooling down entirely will take some billions of years, the nervous person will note that there i 3 really no imniediate causcs for alarm.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
MICROSCOPIC TESTS OF WOOD.

A new line of work, consisting. of the tilicro- scopie examination of wood after it breaks in testing apparatus, has been started by the office of wood utilisation in the. United 'States Forest Service. The structune of wcod is complex. Every species has seveiel different kinds of cells, each of which hes its own size and form. There is also a wide variation in the, number and arrangement of the cells in different species. These differences in struc- ture have their bearing on the strength of the wood. For some time past the Forest Service has been carrying on a Large number of tests on many kinds of wood in order to determine their strength, stiffness, elasticity, and other physical properties. It is not expected that laymen will understand the significance of the propcsed micrescopic investigations .as quickly as architects, builders, and other wood mere.

PHOSPHORESCENCE OF PHOSPHORUS.

It has been shewn by Dr. E. Sha.rff that the phosphorescence of phosphorus is due to oxidation, with the formation of a trioxide of phosphorus. The phenomenon is intermittent, and may be made reversible, says Know- ledge, by increasing the pressure of oxygen beyond a certain point, the phosphorus then becoming non-luminous. If the phosphorus be placed in oxygen 'under a pressure just in- sufficient to extinguish the phosphorescence the trioxide vapour forms a mixture with the oxygen which ignites • spontaneously, and a fresh portion of this inflammable mixture is then slowly formed again. By reducing the pressure of the oxygen the successive forma- tion: of the mixture and its combustion occur at shorter intervals, so that finally the light appears continuous. At this stage there is a rapid increase in temperature' and the phos- phorus takes fire. But this may be checked by once more increasing the pressure of the oxygen, and the phosphorescence then dies out.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
A FOUR-EYED FISH.

A certain fresh-water fish-classed among the Cyprinodonts, . and met with in the rivers of the lowlands of Central America—possesses eyes so extraordinarily constructed that they are fitted net only for seeing at a distance, and in the air, but also for close vision below the water surface. The whole of the eyeball (which) protrudes considerably on the upper side of the heady is as freely movable as any ball-and-socket joint. It can be turned in almost any direction, the fish looking up and down, or forward's and backwards. Wheli the eye is turned completely up, all the white of the upper half of the cornea disappears; again, it can be turned down so far that the upper pupil looks quite horizontal, and then the lower apparatus altogether disappears : within the socket. In swimming, the four-, eyed fish incessantly moves its eyes, which, when the creature is near the surface, pro trude above it, the white of the cornea shin- ing conspicuously, a. sure sign that only the upper Or air-eye is then in use.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
TIDAL WAVES INSIDE THE EARTH.

That the attraction of the sun and moon not only causes tides in the ocean but has a corresponding influence on the solid earth itself has been proved by Dr. 0. Hecker, a Prussian physicist. A quarter of a century ago Pr(Wesson G. H. Darwin concluded that there must be tides in the solid earth as well as in the fluid seas, but he was unabW to de- tect them, and his only conclusion was that the earth's mass was very rigid—at least as rigid as steel. Dr. Hecker now confirms this conclusion, says • Science Sifting.!, but with his improved instruments and methods he has been able to detect and measure the amount by which the moon pulls the solid earth out of shape twice a day. Dr. Ilecker's measure- mcntS, which have lasted continuously more than two years, were to ascertain the lunar influence on the earth's gravity by observing the oscillations of a delicate pendulum. It was by comparing his results with the figures sa calculated for a rigid earth that lie was able to detect the tidal deformation caused by the pull of both moon and sun. Tha series of observations have now established the exist- ence of lunar and solar tidal waves in the solid but flexible mass of the globe.

ULTRA-VIOLET RAYS AND THE EYES.

The action of ultra-violet rays on the human eye has been much discussed ever since Doctors F. Schanz and K. StockhauFen at the Congress of German Naturalists held at Dresden drew attention to the matter. An- other soiinutterest.inig _ ihc.:natruiebsutitoion h n taoswabredesn given by the same men in a leeturc delivered-. - ter- fore the sixteenth annual meeting of the As- sociation of German Electricians, at Erfurt.

Investigations carried out shew that ophthalmia eleetrica is due to ultra-violet rays. The some disease, though in a lees char. acteristie form, is produced also by many other illuminants containing a large percent- age of ultra-violet raiys. Snow blindness is similar in its symptoms to electrical ophthal- mia, and is likewise produced by ultra-violet rays contained in reflected sunlight. That the action of ultra-violet rays, s'o far from being confined to the external eye, also affects its deeper parts, is evidenced by the intense fluorescence of the lens which they are able to produce. The lens thus converts th., short-wave invisible rays into visible rays, acting, as it were, as a

SAFEGUARD TO THE RETINA. As a slight turbidness is further produced in the lens by an intense lighting with ultra- violet rays, it may .be said that the cataract of old age is possibly clue to a progressive alteration of the leas.

The longest ultraviolet spectra are found In electric illuminants, of which the mercury vapour lamp with quartz globe and arc lamps are found to come foremost. Nernst lamps , likewise radiate a considerable percentage of ' ultra-violet rays. In all electric lamps fitted with a glass cover of any kind, the short wave lengths are absorbed by the glass. • The authors draw attention to the fact that blue protective spectacles afford NO PROTECTION AGAINST ULTRA-VIOLET RAYS :, , and that smoke-grey spectacles weaken the whole spectrum, for which reason they are. not suitable for practical purposes. Because . of the special importance of the question, they have undertaken the production of a new kind .of protective glass which is mow", brought On the market under the name of " euphos " glass. This glass, which is of a light greenish-yellow hue, owes its remarkable power of absorbing ultra-violet rays to its special composition rather than to its colour. The compositimi can be adapted to each kind of lamp. With a very slight loss of Belt (3 0 tl per vent.) Lai git‘,3 sen,m3 oil entirely any *dtra-violet rays. peClal experiments made m animals shew tilit if really affords an efa- dent protection to he eye.

NOTES ON GAMES AN3 ATHLETICS. Rugby Football.

",By

Warrington added another to their fine sequence of victories on Saturday, when they were the first League team to win at Runcorn this season. Prior to the match with Warrington, the Linnets had only lost two League games in ten, while the Australians had scored only a narrow victory at their expense. Warrington's recent form sugggested the possibility of victory in the minds of the Wirepullers' supporters, though this was not the view held in Runcorn. How- ever, Warrington just scraped home by 5 points to 3, and recorded their sixth successive victory a remark- able feat considering that only two wins and a draw were gained from the first nine engagements.

The results of Warrington's last six games in their sequence are worth studying. Perpend : For. Against. G. T. P. G. T. T. Merthyr, home St. Helens, away Australia, home 2 210 0 1 3 Swinton, away Oldham, home Runcorn, allay

===== 0002601_19081205_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
13 16 74 1 414

I notice that a good many of the newspapers reports of Satarday's match—written in Runcorn of course suggest that Warrington were lucky to win, and express doubt about the fairness of the try scored by Lees. The game was certainly very keenly and evenly contested, and while the teams seemed so well matched that a draw would have been the fairest result, Warrington undoubtedly possessed a superority at centre three-quarter which won them the match, for Dickenson scored the winning point with a fine drop-kick at a critical stage of the game. Then in regard to the try scored by Lees. The latter appeared to knock the ball on in dropping on it over the line, but it did not matter in the least if he did. The Runcorn t lka forget that there is no such thing as a knock-on over the goal line, and therefore Lees' try was perfectly legitimate whether he knocked the ball forward or not. He was over the line before be reached the ball, and so long as.he was on-side he was perfectly right. The try was scarcely so meritorious as that scored by the home team, but there was cool judgment in the work of both Fish and O'Neill which led up to the point.

As usual in games between Warrington and 11m:corn there was not much open football. The fault for this lies at the door of the Linnets whose backs make a point of lying close up to the scrum in order to prevent any passing by the other side. The method is one which keeps the score down, but in addition to spoiling the game it tends to rough play. There was more tackling than passing, but most of it was quite legitimate. Warrington held their own whether in the scrums or in the loose, and I was very sorry to see Langley and O'Neill sent off for striking. The Warrington International is surely one of the cleanest players under the Northern Union, and deserves as much sympathy as blame, for Langley is a persistent obstructionist who asked for trouble right through the game. Near the end, Woods, a Runcorn forward, I broke his right leg in making a wild kick at the ball in the middle of a Warrington forward rush. This was a most regrettable accident but there was no suspicion of rough play as the cause.

Both teams were handicapped by absentees, the men most missed being Harrison of Runcorn and Brooks of Warrington. For the former Hughes deputised very cleverly at full back, though the cross- wind caused him to put a lot of balls into t,-,uch. Lees re-arreared for Warrington and vas obviously out of condition. He left all the work to Jenkins, end the latter was too well spotted to go far. With Brooks on, there would have. keen a difference in Warrington's back play. I was pleased to see Butter- north play such a good game. Though a veteran, he is not a back number even yet.

Jolley did a great amount of work, but one feature of his tnekinsss at the base of the scrum seemed to be penalised by Providence. In. the second half, Nhen the scores weie level, Jolley was itching to get a goal and with a scrum in the Warrington quarter he saw his opportunity. The ball was being heeled out on the Runcorn side when the artful Jolley pushed it back and got the Warrington halves penalised for if-side. It was an old dodge, but it worked. Unfortu- nately for Runcorn, however, the artful one's kick at goal went yards wide. Warrington rushed to the ether end and obtained the teward of virtue, for Jolley kicked out to Dickensmi who dropped a magnificent goal from quite 40 yards range. By the nay, where has the lengthy George suddenly picked up the knack from ? He has had three shots against Australia, Oldham, and Runcorn— and bit the bull's eye every dime. lhe accomplishment is a useful one. Goal- dropping in Warrington has been a lost art since the days of T. Barnes and J. Cross.

NEWTON-LE-WILLOW'S ARGUMENT.

Warrington are honoured by the inclusion of Brooks and Dickenson in the first test team to meet the Kangaroos at the Park Royal, London, on Saturday next. The selected team is :- H. Gifford (Barrow), back; W. Batten (Hunslet), B. Jenkins (Wigan), G. F. Dickenson, (Warrington), G. Tyson (Oldham), three-quarters; E. Brooks (Warrington), and J. Thomas (Wigan), half backs; W. Jukes (Hunslet), A. Mann (Bradford), A. Robinson (Halifax), W. Longworth (Oldham), J. Higson (Hunslet), and A. Smith (Oldham), forwards.

Hockey. Aitrincham St. Georges proved too good for Latch- fordJast Saturday at Altrincham and won 4-1. The visiting defence was not as steady as usual and the forwards rather disjointed, the ground rendering accurate dribbling and passing almost impossible. The St. Georges forwards are a rasping set and without doubt the best team won. Banks scored Latchford's goal.

The Second Elevens met at Latchford and in a somewhat tame game Latchford won 1-0. Knight shot the only goal scored. Tole played a sound game at centre half.

To-day Clayton come to Latchford and will meet the following First Eleven :—Goals, A. N. Other ; C. Plinston, T. Dixon, backs ; F. Newell, J. B. Wilkinson (captain), J. Albinson, halves ; D. It is always helpful to find others arguing on the ' same side as ourselves. Here is a fine support from Newton-le-Willows of an argument that many of us here have used. This is what Newton-le-Willows says. "I was troubled with inflammation of the bladder, and backache, for six years," says Mrs. A. Chadwick, 41, Mill-lane, Newton-le-Willows; "a bad confinement caused the a ihnents, and from then I suffered off and on. Every cold I caught, or any extra work I did, brought on the troubles ; sometimes I would be laid up, once being in bed a fortnight. "The pains in my back struck me like a knite-thrnst, about every twenty minutes, or if I moved suddenly : there was a constant pain as well. I used to apply warm flannels, which eased me somewhat. The urinary secretions were rather thick, and very painful. "I always felt very wearied and drowsy, and also nervous. I had headaches, giddiness, and terrible palpitation of the heart if I was flurried, or when I went upstairs. I was also short of wind, there was a dazzling befdre my eves, and my sight was poor. I used Doan's pills, which have proved splendid, better than anything I ever used. They acted well upon my kidneys, and then ease in the other symptoms followed ; I have been very woll since. I did a hard day's work the other day, and was surprised at not feeling any ill effects, which proved to me how much good Doan's pills bad done me. (Signed) M. A Chadwick." • Doan's Backache Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence.) Of all chemists and stores, or post free, direct from Foster McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Orford-street, London, W. Mrs. Chadwick was cured hy'Doan's ; be sure that vou. get" DOAN'S."

, Suspected of Poaching.

At the Borough Police Court, on -.Tuesday, Isaac Smith, 10, Morley-street, was summoned for being suspected of coming from land after trespassing in search of game. Defendant admitted having been on the land, but denied having been in search of game.— P.S. Stewart stated that about 6 o'clock on Sunday morning, the 22nd of last month, he was on duty in Delamere-street in company with P.C. Newey when he saw the defendant coining across a field from the direction of the Bewsey estate. They waited till he was in the street, and then stopped him and searched him. They found a gun (produced) in his pocket, but he had no game or powder in his possession. He was wet thrrugh and it seemed as if be had teen out all night. He told the Sergeant that he had been out for a few shots with his pals, who had gone home in another direction. -In answer to the charge the defendant stated that he never tried to run away. Ile got the gun from a Juan, whom he knew and who was bad in bed at that time, and he was comic that way home. He could not have been shooting because ho bad no powder—Sergeant Stewart suggested that defendant could have thrown hie.. powder away before he was searched.—The tungis- ' trates ordered defendant to pay 10s. including costs- cr 11 claws in default.

The funeral is to I take place on a bleak hillside to-morrow, and I ad certain that that hillside will be as cold and wet, and the parson as cold and dry—(loud laughter)—as Scotch 1 hillsides at this season of the year, and Scotch p irsons at all seasons are. (More laughter.) lam certain also that he will hive neither compunction nor compassion. He will give us a lung prayer, a longer exhortation, and a still longer sermon on the shortness and the brevity of human life,—my relative was ninety-two." (Much laughter). Well, if for a few minutes we are to take a circular tour from West to East of Bonnie Scotland it is a little difficult to know where to begin, because from Macrahanish, that far famed golf links at the Mull of Cautyre, the whole Coast lino of Scotland up the Western Highlands is full of infinite beauty. But lam bound to confess that the beauty of the scenery is not the only attraction which has lured me many a time to that part of the country to try my luck with the finny darters there. If I remember right the very first fresh- run silvery sea trout I ever had on my line was at Shiel at the head of Loch Duich, where I had one splendid day's sport ef ter a real good spate in the highlands. Loch Duich, as many Scotchmen here, who know their beloved country better than I can pretend to, are well aware, runs parallel to the famous Loch bourn, and is adjacent to the wild beauty of Skye. So let us take the steamer through the narrows of Kyle Akin pass Broadford on to Sliohagan, and from there walk and climb to the point where all at once there burst on the view the splendid semi-circle of rocky peaks with Scuir na Gillean—" The Peak of the Stag's Antlers," as I believe that means in English—at their head, and gloomy Loch Coruisk below them. I have bad the good fortune to see much of grandeur and beauty in the mountain scenery of the Alps, the Tyrol, the Pyrenees at different times, but to my mind little that I have seen can equal and nothing surpass ' the same combination of beauty and grandeur in the Northern and Western Highlands, and of mountain scenery there surely the Coolins are the most striking. One calls to mind Scott's famous lines about them, " Stranger, if e'er thine ardent steps have trod The Northern realms of ancient Caledon, Where the proud queen of wilderness hath placed, By glen and cataract her lonely throne, Sublime, yet sad delight thy soul bath known, Gazing on pathless glen and mountain high, Listing where from the cliffs the torrents thrown Mingle their echoes with the eagles' cry, And with the sounding lake, and with the moaning sky." Scott then refers to Loch Rannock and some other places famous for their beauty in that part of the world, and then he goes on to say : "But be the minstrel judge, they yield the prize,. For desert dignity to that dread shore , Which sees grim Coolin rise And hears Coruskin roar."—(Applause.) Continuing our circular tour we wend our way over the placid inland sea northwards through historic ground once traversed by bonnie Prince Charlie and Flora McDonald, till by the Northern Gairloch we reach Loch Mares+, which my father used to think the most beautiful of all the lochs of Scotland, under the mighty shadow of old Shiehallian. After another turn there at the sea trout—(laughter)—we cut across to the East Coast, and there for a moment let us halt at Aberdeen, as I halted there Once many years ago. lam bound to say that on that occasion I experienced there, as in other parts of Scotland, the most perfect hospitality, and I cannot for the life of me understand —perhaps Mr. Chairman, some of our friends will explain the matter to us—why that city of granite rock and granite character should be so misunderstood. There are several stories told of Aberdonians and let me give you one as a sample. I know that our Scotch friends here will think it the height of folly and presumption for a mere inferior Southron—(laughter) —to attempt to imitate their rich and sonorous dialect. (Laughter.) I suppose I am more or less tempting Providence. However, Mr. Chairman, I am going to risk it, and the story is of our old friend Saundy— (laughter)—who went to call on McPherson. Ills host invited him to have a glass of whiskey, and Saundy at once expressed his willingness to take a glass, which McPherson forthwith began to pour out. The rest of the story I must tell you in Saundy's own words— "As McPherson poured out I said,' Stop ! stop, Mon !' 'oot aft mere civeelity ' • and domme, but he stoppit !" (Much laughter.) Mr. Crosfleld also told another story about Aberdonians which caused much amusement, and he added that he need scarcely say he entirely dis- associated himself, as he was also sure every good Scot would do, from any such reflection on Aberdonian hospitality. They had certainly had no such stinted measure at their fine banquet that evening. (Hear, hear and laughter.) Travelling down the East Coast one reached the grey old city by the sea, that veritable Mecca for golfers to which all devotees of the Royal and Ancient gime ever turned their eyes. " There is a something about St. Andrew's " continued Mr. Crosfleld, "you get nowhere else. You feel it the moment you get out at Leuchars Junction and get your first peep of the distant towers and ruins of the old place. I should be tempted to say it has an atmosphere of its own, hut for the fact that I am not going to drift into any discussion on polities or the education problem,notwithstanding the sly attempt made by my friend Mr. Hall to lure me on to that. But the charm of St. Andrew's is something more than the attractive character of the wondef fully sporting golf one gets there, and the honourable company of golfers who gather there year by year, especially for the great meeting which is at the end of September. It is some- thing mere than that for the tradition bud rofrance of the place plays its part among the ruins of the old cathedral, the Castle and other ruined battlements where " Dim with the mist of years, gray flits the shade of goner." (Applause). The other great golfing centre of the North is, of course, the remarkable variety of fine golf links which you find in the neighbourhood of North Berwick and the hiss Rock, and I am sure that we in Old England , are under a permanent obligation to our friends from the North who by providing these attractions for us in their own country, and by their example have set us to work at one of the most popular—l had almost said the most popular—out-door game of modern times. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Chairmen, it gives me great pleasure to invite this distinguished company to drink now to this toast. lam delighted that the calls on the Society are low, and that on the other hand more than once, and in more than one respect Warrington has benefited by your support and geuerous contributions. I trust that may always continue to be the case and that alike for social and charitable purposes this important society may continue to prosper and greatly flourish. (Applause.) I ask you gentlemen to drink with me to that toast. (Applause.) Mr. G. BOWMAN (hon. treasurer) responded. He said their objects were two-fold—charity and sociability —and he was certain that they had carried out their fundamental principles since the inauguration of the society. Not only did they assist fellow Scots who needed help, but they also did something for local charities. (Applause). "THE CORPORATION AND TOWN OF WA R RIN GTO N." THE MAYOR AND TIIE BRIDGE. Mr. R. LOYAL TOASTS.

The PRESIDENT in a very happy speech proposed the 7 toast of " The King." He referred to the great trans- ' formation whic'o had came over the relationships of England and Scotland in comparatively recent history and said the two natioux had now well amalga- mated. The toast was enthusiastically honoured as were also those of " The Queen," and " The Duke and Duchess of Rothsay." "NAVY, ARMY, AND RESERVE FORCES." Mr. J. L. DAVENPORT, Vice-President, in submitting this toast, said that so long as the supremacy of the -firs's line of defence was not seriously endangered, and if the far-reaching changes which had bees made in the organization and equipment of our regular and terri- torial forced produced the results expected of them, we might still with untroubled minds continuo in the even tenur of our way and feel absolutely secure in the enjoyment of all our privileges. But we must not live in a fool's paradise. With other nations having ambitions and aspirations elbowing their way forward, we must see that our interests were not suffer- ing or being pushed aside, and that every branch of the service was kept in the highest state of efficiency. Preparedness for war was the greatest guarantee of Peace. He considered this above party considerations, but the utterances of leading politicians on both sides Of the House of Commons had been most reassuring especially with respect to the Navy. (Applause.) Colonel J. D. FAIRCLOUGII, replying, said Europe had been passing through a critical period. They had bad rumours of wars, and if a war broke out in Europe ' they never knew who might be dragged into it. lie was pleased to see that Mr. Asquith had said our Navy was to be equal to the material of any two of the strongest powers. We must have supremacy on the sea or the whole fabric of our Empire would fall to Pieces. A strong Navy to us was as vital as a strong Army to a continental nation. Mr. Balfour a few years age said invasion was a thing that was not seriously to be talked about, but only the other day they had a Speech from an old soldier who said we wanted a million Men to defend us against invasion. When Mr. Haldane started his Territorial scheme he fixed the number of Men required at 900,000. That was afterwards reduced to 300,000, and we had not got them yet. Uutil we had some systematic training by which Mr. Haldane's excel- lent frame-work could be carried out, we never would he quite safe. It was quite possible our fleet might be temporarily crippled, and it did not need the absolute Command of the sea to bring a large number of men across to this country. If England were conquered the indemnity would be tremendous, and it would be woe to ans nation that was conquered in the Twentieth 'Century. (Applause). .. 'THE CtERGY." Mr. Mara as Str,ll.-2ZER proposed" The clergy," whom be described as the soldiers of peace—men who were -ever ready to fight for right against wrong, for good, against evil, and for the welfare and uplifting of common humanity. He expressed the gratification of Scotehreen at the recent appointment to +he Arch- . bishopric of York—an appointment which had been - generally well received and which they trusted would t -Prove to have been well deserved. (Applause.) • The Remo, in responding, referred to the great ' impartiality of those Ministers in high places upon _ whom rested the responsibility of making the most ' important appointments for the good of the Church. ' -There were some who did tact like the appointments * to rest with the 'tarp Minister but he must say that be honestly did, Milluse after all the appointment was : Simply a nomination and they might be quite sure that -the Prime Minister for his own sake as well as for the : sake of the countiy would try to nominate the best ' !Ilan. Having referred to the distinguished positions ' 'in the Church which were occupied by Scotchmen, the ' rirstor alluded to the great independence of the clergy. There was no collusion between one clergyman 'f. and another and he thought the best illustration of - that he could give was that all the Bishops, generally 1 ssPeaking, were in favour of the Licensing Bill and the 1: -Education Bill but the inferior clergy were opposed. r, (Laughter.) It was cause for congratulation that such F., good feeling existed between the clergy and ministers L nf all denominations. (Applause.) "BONNIE SCOTLAND." • Mr. E. J. HALL, in a racy and humorous speech, sub- k ra, itted the toast of the evening, that of " Bonnie f3cotland." Having given many instances of the charm- mg use of the charming word "bonny "in Scotland, he touched upon the antiquity of Scotland and briefly Sketched the traditional history of the " Stone of ' destiny"now in Westminster Abbey. Continuing Mr. Hall said : We come to almost modern times when we read , af the building of "Hadrian's Wall," built by the . siemens for the purpose of keeping the Scots out of • ' Englmld. I read the other night in a fanny book, which has recently been published, a remark which I . thought rather rude, to the effect that "fragments of • Hadrian's Wall still remain, but it no longer answers its original purpose." (Laughter and applause.) The ~ "me book refers to the" Robber Caves," which used to : abound in the Highlands, and adds that 'Hwy no longer . exist, but that their place has been taken in Scotland by the modern hotel. (Lanehter.) Lord Macaulay ' writing about the state of Scotland, soon after the Restoration, writes:—" The administration of Scotland Was in Scottish hands ; for no Englishmen had any -,. motive to emigrate northwards and to contend with 1• : the shrewdest Dud most pertinacious of all races for What was to be scraped together in the poorest 'of all treasuries." Times change, and we have lived I'o see a pilgrimage to the north, in politics if not in ' cornme•ce. Mr. Winston Churchill is only the last to ' " follow his leader " over the border, and it is fast • becoming a question whether at any rate one party in Hie State will not before long have to ferns its adminis- . ttations almost entirely from among Scots or b:nglish- - Men sitting for Scottish constituencies. (Laughter.) : Tile hen. member for Warrington has begun to cast longing eyes towards bonny Scotland. He only con- ' fesses to dreams of " trout streams " at present, but I who knows ? (Laughter.) Will you, and he, however, ' ' alltaw me to express this personal hope, that, in the ' Main, whether be continues to be member for Warring- '. ton or not, he will, like his predecessor, Mr. Pierpoint, Stick to Warrinetcn. He regretted to notice that many of the picturesque old sigas,which were extremely interesting, had vanished from their inns. He would like to see them revived and were he a member of the Licensing Committee he would suggest that anybody applying for a licence should be asked to put up the old-fashioned sign so that people could FMC what the house meant. Ile regretted that the trade of Watrington was not what they all desired, but they might con- gratulate themselves that the depression was nothing like so had as in other towns. (Hear, hear). He looked forward to the time—he hoped it would come soon— when the townspeople would be in full work and the chimneys pouring out their horrible smoke—(hear, hear)—and when W'arringtou would be, like the rest of I England, prosperous. L The MAYOR,in responding,said he wanted the Scotch- men present to make their mark in Warrington by supporting and assisting him during his term of (ace to get a new Warrington Bridge, and one that would be worthy of the town and its people. (Applause.) It must be at least 80ft, wide, because of the great and ever-increasing tratlic going on in that part of the town. He would certainly build a substantial bridge, and one that would be an everlasting monument to the town. He believed an iron bridge would be preferable t 3 a ferro-concrete one, because, as be understood, the latter was liable to crack and would give a lot of trouble, but upon that point, of course, they would have to get an expert engineer's advice. (Hear, hear.) The building of the bridge would give a great deal of employment to the people, and if they commenced it at once they would save thousands of pounds in the buying of iron, as he understood it was now very cheap, in fact, that it was never cheaper. (Applause.) There were many other matters which required the attention of the Town Council, but he wished to make the now bridge the most important ooe. With regard to the Corporation generally, he could tell that gathering with the most perfect confidence that they were a body of men who intended to do the best they could for the town in which they lived. The duties were by no means and he believed that all the councillors present would support him when he said they were actuated with a desire to make this the best-governed town in Lanca- shire. (Applause.) The Corporation were going to Parliament to regulate the signs about which Mr. Pier- point had been speaking. (Applause). In conclusion the Mayor read the following letter 35, Arpley- street, Warrington, 29th November, 1903. Dear Mr. Mayor,—While reading to-day the report of the Nursing Association's annual meeting F have been singularly reminded of the old days when you and I were assisting in choir work at 'St. Ann's Church ; because just across the way, you will remember was the Guild House presided over by Mr. John White, in connection with which some zealous Church folk (including the late James Bolton and Hamlet Houghton) maintained for many years a trained nurse, whose ser- vices proved an immense blessing to that crowded parish. It is in happy memory of those days that I send you the accompanying cheque to clear off the Nursing Association's adverse balance of £O2 I Is. li. T am grad to read that so energetic a man as Mr. Henry Roberts has consented to become the new treasurer.— I am, dear Mr. Mayor, yours very truly, WILLIAM JoIINSCAN." (A ppla use.)

Alderman BOLTON, in reply, said that if Councillor Peacock had given them Aladdin's lamp something could have been done, but bowling greens bad to be made in the winter. Before they could prepare a bowl- ing green they must have money for the purpose, and It must be borrowed from the Local Government Board. The committee wanted a reasoaable time allowed them in which to do the work, as the necessary formalities had to be gone through and the plans prepared. They must apply for £4,000 or £5,000 for the bowling greens. I The committee would do what they could in the matter. The minutes were then agreed to. WATERWORKS PROFITS. IMPENDING BIG SCHEME. Alderman EVANS, proposing the minutes of the Water Committee, made an important statement. He said for the first time for a nuaiber of years they had a balance on the right side of their account. (Hear, hear.) For the year ending March 31st last there was a loss of £1,047. This half year showed a net profit of £531, which was very gratyfying.. In addition the General Purposes Committee had kindly allowed the sum of £317, the cost of the opposition to the Liverpool Bill, to be transferred to the Parliamentary Fund, so that brought the profit to over £9OO. Expensive Scheme in Prospect.

MEALS FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN. THE COST OF THE APPLIANCES.

In the absence of Councillor Fulton-Smith, Alderman ELusoN moved the confirmation of those recommenda- tions of the Education Committee which required the confirmation of the Council.

Councillor Mutlssos seconded. Councillor EVANS roved as an amendment that the following minute of the Education Committee be not confirmed, viz., " On the recommendation of the sub- committee appointed to consider the arrangements for putting into force the Education (Provision of Meals). Act, 1906, it was resolved (a) that further particulars as to the cost of the boilers required be obtained ; (b) that an 'oveu be purchns•A at a cost of £l9 lfas. ; (o) that a boiler, cylinder and tank for the supply of hot water be obtained at a cost of .C 7 61. 6d. ' • and (d) that authority be given for the purchase of the folloning —Three cans for the conveyance of soup, &c., at a cost not exceeding £4 10s. 30 dozen spoons Cl a cost of £6 103. ; and 30 dozen plates at a cost of £1 is. 60." He wanted to know what kind of an oven WM required and for what purpose. He had yet to learn that dinners had to be provided as well as breakfasts. (Hear, hear.) The sum of £l9 lss. was only ss. short of the amount at which the byelaws required tenders to be advertised for. Was it necessary to spend £4 10a. on cans and to provide spoons which cost 4s. 4d. a dozen? Decent spoons could be obtained at is. 101. a dozen ; 2s. at the outs' de. _ _

Councillor PEMBERTON seconded and said the prices seemed excessive. Ile would like to know what kind of spoons could be obtained for Is. 10s. a dozen. Councillor EVANS : I can show on one. (Laughter.) He 'then took a spoon out of his pocket and it was handed to Conncillor Pemberton. .

Alderman BOLTON said it was wrong to go in for elaborate and expensive appliances. The sum of £lOO was being asked for the provision of food, and £lO or £5O for appliances, and it seemed to him that it was going to be an expensive business. Councillor LORD said he was told the spoon produced by Councillor Evans could be supplied at 10d. a dozen, and there was a vast difference between 10d. and 4s. Id. A gentleman present had offered to supply as many of those spoons as they liked at 10d. a dozen. The amendment was carried, and the other minutes requiring confirmation were approved.

LIVERPOOL UNIVERSITY.

Alderman BENNETT was re-appointed a representative on the governing body of the Liverpool University for three years.

WESL-EYAN TRUSLEES AND SUNDAY TRADING. At a meeting of the Watch committee a letter was read from the trustees of the Latchford Wesleyan Church with regard to Sunday trading and the noise caused by children outside their church, and, the matter was referred to the Chief Constable.

The minutes were approved, on the motion of the MAYOR, seconded by the DEPUTY-MAYOR. HOWARD PLACE: A SUGGESTED BE kUTY SPOT. At a meeting of the Street improvement and Lighting Committee, a letter was read from the shop- keepers, and res:dents in Howard-place, complaining of insufficient lighting and the annoyance caused by children and others playing on the open space; and it was resolved, that the question of lighting he referred to the Lighting Sub-committee, and that the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee, be requested to take over the open space and to snrround the same with an unclimbable fence. _ .

Alderman BENNETT moved the confirmation of the minutes, and said be was not in accord with the com- mittee with reference to Howard-place. A complaint was made about the annoyance caused by children playing on the open space, but they had nowhere else to go and if the space were filled with trees and tiowera the conditions would be even worse from that point of view. Tf the resolution were carried be hoped a nice job would be made of the place, which might be converted into one of the local beauty spots with a border of trees and a status in the centre. Whilst he had every sympathy with the shopkeepers he had a con- siderable amount also for the poor little gutter sparrows who had nowhere else to play except in the public- place which had been named. Councillor ROBF,RTS : Howley Bridge. Alderman BENNETT Howley Bridge is like a dissolving view. We seem to think we have got it and it suddenly fades into thin air. (Laughter.) Councillor PENIBERTON said he thought the shop- keepers had a great cause for complaint, and they ought to be protected. It would, however, be a mistake to have the land fenced round entirely. Many flowers would not grow in Howard-place. Alderman BENNETT said that tvhen the old police station was in existence one of the ideals they had was that it should be pulled down and that an open space should be preserved ; but if they fenced it in the idea they had was practically nullified. He should like to see trees provided with proper guards around them and a statue in the centre of the place. Alderman Bor.ros : Pot you on the top. (Laughter). Alderman BENNETT : I Would sooner make way for a much abler man. He suggested it would be an appro- priate .pot on which to place a statue of John Howard, and he hoped some eminent citizen would present the town with one. • At a later stage of the meeting, when the minutes of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committae came up for consideration ! Aldermen BOLTON referred to the same subject on the following resolution of the committee :—" The Street Improvement and Lighting Committee having requested this committee to take over the open space in fioward-place with a view to it being laid out, it was resolved that the open space be taken over, and that the Borough Surveyor be requested to report on the laying out thereof." The Alderman said the tubs would disappear and railings would be provided to pro- tect the trees while they were growing, but these were matters of detail. They had a collection of brickbats under the surface at the present time. Councillor PEatitureroN said that before the land was laid out the General Purposes Committee should have an opportunity of knowing how that was to he done.

Mr. BALFOUR has once more "THE EDUC4TION triumphed The Bill of BILL DEAD! Mr. RUNCIMAN, a brave attempt to temporarily -settle the Education question, will on Monday be withdrawn. For the sake of t'ae children, who may be kept innocent victims of a lamentable and unchristian wrangle for another generation, we deeply deplore this result of a degrading struggle for which we have to thank the Tory leader and such hot-headed party extremists as the BISHOP of Mcicass.rza. The latter at the Church Council meeting in London on Toursday " expressed his belief that each time they rejected the GJvern- meat proposals they gat something better next time " ; he thus sought the destruction of the Bill, as did another Right Reverend prelate, who asserted that "the success of the Bill would mean the triumph of the principles that all schools should be under public control, with no tests for teachers, principles against which they had always protested with all their force." In saying this, the BISHOP OF BEVERLEY was undermining All chance of compromise,for both the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY and Mr. BeLvona had repeatedly stated that those two principles must now prevail. Clearly the bill has failed on the question of the fcash contributions to "contracting-out " 'Church schools! With the baser kind of prelate, such as D. Krvox, this object of the Church is not one to be ashamed of ; he hAs impudently asserted that " So long as plenty of money found its way to the Church of E Igland they might net quarrel r The Primate's idols are not so 'bluntly squalid. Nevetheless, while the Govern- ment have desired contracting out 'to be the exception, he and those behind him have sought to make it the rule, and have, as Mr. RuNcntAN indicated yesterday afternoon, demanded financial support altogether in excess of what justice 'reclaim:3, or the G wernment in view of its con- sistency and its pledges in the country, could consent to accerle. List week, Mr. BALF3Ineg lordly minions slew

- the Gwernrnent Licensing Bill, as they had slain

111 r. BICIRMA:S Education MU two years before, and as they had thrown ont other first-cless measures in the interval. This week the Tory lead3rs' priestly minions have rendered futile an effort of the Government to settle for a time, one of the same urgent questions by a compromise

with clerical representatives of the same party minority. We see a reasonable measure defeated by the grasping greed of leaders of the wealthy Bitablished Church, with the object of getting their hands deeper and deeper into the public pocket for the support and the extension of their sectarian ends. Surely the Government Will not longer hesitate ! Their duty seems to be plain ! Lit them at once appeal to the people for whom they have so heroically striven, and claim, as they confidently may, their unhesitating support in finally taking such measures as shall render it impossible any

longer for the irresponsible House of Lards to impede the country in its determination to establish aids to and rarnove stumblings-blocks Trona the path of its peaceful advancement.

We notice that the Tory DEFILING THE triumph at Chelmsford was WELLS. immediately hailed by the Chamberlainites (among whom we suppose we must now include Mr. BALroon and Lord LA NSDOWN E) aa a Tariff

Reform victory. To ourselves it does not seem

that it can with propriety be so described. The auccessful candidate says that it was a victory for tinionisra, then for protecting the Irish loyalists, and then for a strong navy. Tariff reform comes tart in his enumeration of the causes which have

===== 0002601_19081205_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
returned him to Parliament by a majority which is ikely to ensure for him a long po3session of his seat The style of his reference to a strong navy indicates better than anything else, perhaps, why 11-4. has been returned by a majority of between two and three thousand. To say that " Chelms- ford is in favour of a strong navy," and to add that this is proved by the result of the election is really to announce that the successful candidate and his friends have been successful in persual- ing the electors that the present Government is in favour of a weak navy, which, as we all know, is untrue. It is unfortunately the case that an accurate representation of the facts in respect of anything whatever is coming to be more and more disregarded by the Tory party at election times.

The fight in all recent instances has taken place on false issues. Matters which should have been of common knowledge have been misrepre- sented with deliberation and with a purpose. We take this pretension about " a strong navy " as a typical example. Tnis surely ahould be the most unfitting of all times for suggesting that the navy is being neglected, or that its strength is likely to be diminished, or that it has not been maintained by the Liberal Government on the level assumed to have been reached by its predecessor. The reverse of all this is true, and should be notorionv. The Government is spending more money on the navy than was spent on it by Mr. Ranrouit's adminis- tration, and it is getting more value for every pound spent. The navy was never so strong as it is at the present moment. The PRIME MINISTER has declared in favour of a two-Power standard in terms more decided and more clear than have ever before been employed in Parliament. We are actually, as was shown not many days since, much nearer a Four-Power than a Two-Power standard. What then, is meant by the repre- sentation that voting for a Tory candidate is the same thing as voting for a strong navy ? Nothing more, we fear, is intended than to deceive the electors into voting on a falst issue. We have, as we believe, stoicism enough to bear a defeat with equanimity. We do not cry out because we have been beaten. We cannot help desiring, however, not on party, but on truly national grounds, that questions agitating the national mind should be placed before the elec- tors with something approaching to common fairness. If politics were merely a game it would not matter what was said or whether this candidate or the other were successful. The truth is, how- ever, that some of the questions now put to issue at elections are vital to the life of the nation. They should be decided, therefore, in a square, stand-up fight. There are wide grounds for honest differences of opinion. If political contests took place about these alone there could be no just ground tor complaint. This remark applies even to Tariff Reform, which is said to be the deciding course of the Chel.nsford victory. No sincere Free Trader could be in fear of the result if the cause were to be argued. He would depend on the strength of his principles and the incontrovertible nature of the facts by which they are supported. But if the facts are to be systematically distorted, if deliberately wrong representations are to be made to electors, not in a position to be well informed, then the wells of truth are being poisoned, not with the result only of returning one man instead of another to the House of Commons, but with the tremendously greater consequence of undermining the national welfare and the stability of the Empire.

The most exacting elector Tun BOROUGH could not cavil at the MEN.lnsit's ACTIVITY. attention Mr. Crosbield has

paid of late both to his constituents and to his constituency. Not only has he been in close attendance at the House of Commons when in London, but he has taken every opportunity of appearing at public functions in Warrington and speaking in that happy, yet thoughtful strain, which now seems so natural to him. Whether among the bazaars or bowlers, the politicians or the Caledonians, he has been equally at home. He was perhaps not heard to the best advantage at the great Liberal demonstration on Saturday evening', but Mr. Crosfield then spoke under the double disadvantage of fallowing Mr. Birrell, and of suffering from the effects of a cold which had weakened his voice considerably. On Monday, however, the genial company of Scots assembled to celebrate St. Andrew's day, found him in fine form. The running fire of raillery between Mr. Crosfield and Mr. Hall, spiced with sly political allusion, was greatly enjoyed, and we hope our readers will turn to page 3 of this issue and read the report printed there. The Member has spent many happy days North of the Tweed and it was high praise indeed which he bestowed on ' bonny Scotland's charms : "I have had the good fortune to see much of grandeur and beauty in the mountain scenery of the Alps, the Tyrol, the Pyrenees at different times, but to my mind little I have seen can equal and nothing surpass the same combination of beauty and grandeur in the Northern and Western Highlands." As a golfer, too, Mr. Crosfield had much to say about that veritable Mecca for devotees of the sport—St. Andrew's—and his speech will not readily be forgotten. This will probably be his last oration in Warrington for a month or two, as he intencis shortly to pay a visit to the South of France and enjoy a holiday, with the added satisfaction that it is well-earned.

Warrington Liberals will MR. BIRRELL'S long retain the pleasantest Visa. recollections of Mr. Birrell's

visit, and we trust that he, too, will be gratified at the renewal of acquain• tanceship even after 60 long a lapse as forty years. Mr. Birrell was forty years younger when he last visited the old town, and it is to be hoped that he will not be forty years older before he comes again. Even the best of memories and the tenderest of recollections are liable to be somewhat weakened by such an interval. If he was over-due, however, Mr. Birrell quite made up for even an unwitting neglect by the character of his speech at the great Liberal demonstration in the Parr Hall on Saturday evening. Much is expected from a Cabinet Minister, and particularly from one with such a record of political and literary achievements as Mr. Birrell possesses. If the occasion was momentous when Mr. Birrell listened at the feet of Gladstone on the occasion of his first visit to Warrington, it was even more so on Saturday evening, for the Liberal Govern- ment, was, and is, face to face with a crisis which is sure to become historical. The cold-blooded murder of the Licensing Bill by the hereditary and irresponsible House of Lords has awakened the people to a realisation of the fact that, while a Second Chamber is constitutionally a valuable institution, such a Chamber as that dominated by the Peers, and operated solely by the mechanism of the Tory party,is a menance to the welfare and progress of the nation. Mr. Birrell is " a Second Chamber man" but he was bound to confess that "we have here a Second Chamber. . .which has abdicated all its functions and is no longer entitled to receive that consideration which is due Ito a Second Chamber which properly conducts its business." Though speaking with the reserve incumbent upon a man holding Cabinet rank, Mr. Birrell faced the situation boldly and bade the House of Lords an uncompromising defiance on behalf of the Liberal Party and the allied forces of progress. The guillotining of the Licensing Bill with no more trial or hearing than that accorded

===== 0002601_19081205_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner Saturday, December 5, 1908.

the unfortunate aristocrat victims of the French Revolution,, does not terminate the Government's schemes for giving temperance legislation a considerable forward heave. The Bill "is our Bill " said Mr. Birrell, " and our Bill it will remain until it becomes your law." He made it clear that now the sword had been drawn between the people and the Peers the scabbard could be thrown away. It is a fight a routrance, and though the means either to end or mend the Lords are not ready to hand, the struggle having com- menced can only terminate when the people have prevailed. And is not Providence understood to favour the big battalions ?

Mr. Birrell appeared first in his chief role of Secretary of State for Ireland. It has already been said by Mr. Redmond, the Leader of the Irish Parliamentary Party, that Mr. Birrell is the best Irish Secretary they have ever had. Such a testimony is not to be despised, for Irishmen are not prone to pour blessings on the heads of the gentlemen who occupy this distinguished but harassing post. The Chief Secretary is usually the butt at which to hurl the envenomed darts of those Irishmen to whom government by proxy is an insult and nothing more. Mr. Birrell has already won the respect and the love of Irishmen on both sides of St. George's Channel, even if he has not yet solved the problems which so grievously burden the " disthressful counthry." The reason was apparent to those who heard Mr. Birrell's speech on Saturday. Had he been an Irishman born he could not have referred with more tender solicitude to the diffi- calties which beset the country. His politics are those of the heart as well as the mind, and nothing could be clearer than that so far as in him lies Mr. Birrell will,while he holds the reins, improve the lot of the Irish peasantry and hasten the granting of that Home Rule which is still the dream of the patriotic Irishmen and still a vitar essential in English Liberal policy. However much or little Mr. Birrell succeeds in accomplishing as Secretary for Ireland, he will always have the grateful recogni- tion from Irishmen and their sympathisers that he dug down to the root of the poverty in Ireland and introduced a Land Bill fraught with remedial possibilities of the brightest character. This Bill has yet to be considered. We hope it will have a happier fate than its Education and Licensing predecessors.

Dorando's victory in DORA/11)0.8 SECOND America against his old MARATHON. rival in the Olympic Marathon race is popular everywhere. It is just the victory that one would have liked to see. It shows him the better man. The race was run upon a track giving '262 laps to the twenty-six miles, and it was inevitable, with the better going and the hills eliminated, the time should have' been shorter. airando and Hayes, the nominal Olympic winner, ran almost neck and neck until the last mile. The the Italian went ahead and finished with a lead, made in the final mile, of half a lap. He had led the whole way, Hayes made five desperate attempts to take the lead, but Dorando answered spurt with spurt, and ' won the race which he had paced from start to finish. It was a notable achievement. The time, Dirs. 4.lmin. 20 I.ssec' was ten minutes better than his own Windsor time, and eleven minutes better than Hayes' time in the Olympic race. Dorando beat Hayes at Madison-square by three- quarters of a minute. Both men were run out. Sportsmen in this country acclaim the result because of the fine, even the poetic justice which iiivests it, and it possesses the added lustre that Dorando achieved it in the country and amid the friends of a rival who was not to be despised.

A meeting of the members and friends in con- nection with the Sankey Midges Wesleyan Chapel was held after the evening service last Sunday, to bid good-bye to Mr. Joseph Wadsworth, who a short time ago left to undertake new duties at Cadishea.d. The Rev, F.W. Kernick, M.A. occupied the chair and testified to the great pleasure he had had in working with Mr. Wadsworth during his short time in War- rington. Representatives from every branch of the church's work spoke of the intense earnestness with which Mr. Wadsworth did his duties and regretted deeply the necessity for his removal. For ten years he had been closely connected with the work of the church. Eight of these years he had been society steward.—Mr. J. Corradine then presented a purse of gold to Mr. Wadsworth as a token of the esteem in which he and Mrs. Wadsworth are held by the people at Sankey Bridges Chapel. Mr. Wadsworth , replied thanking the friends for their kind words and: actions now and during his term of office.

Warrington Man Killed on the Railway. Mr. T. E. Sampson, Liverpool city coroner, held an inquest on Wednesday on the body of Harry Edward Wheeler, twenty-four years of age, a labourer, who had been engaged at Winwick Lunatic Asylum, War- rington, and who had lodged in Long-lane, Orford, Warrington. Wheeler was found lifeless early on Tuesday morning on the Cheshire Lines Railway, at St. Mary's-road Bridge, Garston, under circumstances which suggested that ho had been struck in the back by an inward-bound train. How he came to be. on the line was a mystery. —Superintendent liamage, of the Cheshire Lines railway police, who had roads inquiries, deposed that no engine-driver or other railway official had seen or heard anything of Wheeler or any other man struck by any engine.—Wheeler had written the following letter to his mother : —" My dear Mother,—Just a line, hoping you are all well. I am sorry to tell you I cannot stand this any longer ;- my head is so had. Forgive me, dear mother, and give my love to all at home. I shall be in Liverpool to-day, where I hope never tn be seen again. God bless you all.—From your loving son, Harry."—An open verdict was returned.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

For once the militant suffragettes have been foiled. They found themselves quite unable on Saturday to carry out their threat of heckling Mr. Birrell and other Cabinet Ministers whenever they speak in public. The orderly meeting in Parr Hall was a feather in the cap of the Liberal Association and its agent, and should do much to encourage other prominent politi cians to select NV4rrington for the delivery o important speeches designed to reach the public ear without senseless interruption.

* Mr. E. J. Tlall in his entertaining speech at the Caledonian Association's dinner on Monday evening gave a graphic account of the supposed history of the famous Stone of Destiny "from Scone which now finds a place in Westminster Abbey. Efe recalled the tradition that this is the stone" which the patriarch Jacob had for a pillow on that memorable night in Luz" and traced its subsequent wanderinge in Egypt, Spain and Ireland. One important fact, however, quite escaped his attention—that it is a piece of Scotch sandstone !

* * * Mr. Birrell's speech at Parr Hall was full of tilos° happy illustrations and flashes of humour for which he is famous. But nothing tickled the audience more than his introduction of the Education question' Referring to his interest in Mr. Runciman's attempt to solve the knotty problem, Mr. Birrell said with all solemnity and innocence: "Like the retired soap- boiler who used to go back to the factory on melting days so that he might sniff the old odours out of which he had made his fortune, I have been hovering round the House of Commons while this education question has been in the air." There was a " soap-boiler "at Mr. Birrell's elbow, and no one laughed more heartily at the speaker's accidental bit than Mr. Crosfield himself.

* * In connection with Mr. Binell's visit, it is worthy of note that it was during the days Mr. Crosfield spent with Mr. Birrell in Switzerland some 15 months ago, and afterwards as his guest at his official resid- ence in Dublin, that the question of a visit to Warrington was discussed and happily settled. Other constituencies, as we happen to know, are envious of the way Warrington has been favoured of late with visits from notable politicians, and would like to know how it is managed. Well, Mr. Crosfield has given away the secret as to how he captured Mr. Birrell. It all amounts to employing the art of persuasion at the proper time and place. We in Warrington know that we have to thank the Borough Member for our fairly frequent political treats, and are profoundly grateful in consequence. *_* * The Warrington Rural District Council is to discuss its relations with the Lancashire County Council on the question of road maintenance. It is contended— and rightly, we think—that with the enormous increase of heavy traffic, the County Council ought to make something like a pro- portionate advance in their contribution to tlie upkeep of the roads. As a matter of fact the District Council is receiving the same amount per mile from the county authorities as when the roads were taken over many years ago. This is obviously unjust for the roads are now so cut tip by heavy motor and other traffic that maintenance is a much more serious item, and we hope the District Council will be able to arrange some more satisfactory sharing of the burden.

* * *- The question "Who is a workman? "—a question which has caused some discussion in Warrington— was raised last reek in the Police Court at Wigan. A solicitor who travelled by a workman's car in the morning claimed a workman's ticket. The conductor insisted on the full fare, and this being refused, the solicitor was summoned. The Bench evidently held that the defendant was in the wrong, for they imposed a fine of a shilling and costs, but agreed to state a case for a superior court. The great difficulty in these cases is where to draw the line, unless it is to be decided by moleskins and corduroys. The London suburban railways tried for long to make the distinc- tion, but had to give it up, and now, within certain specified hours, supply workmen's tickets to all who ask for them. In Warrington none but half fare tickets are issued between certain hours. This would seem to be the wisest plan. At any rate it saves trouble, and the number of " workmen" who are not workmen wishing to travel in the early morning is so small that the game of singling them out seems hardly worth the candle.

ORDERS BY TELEPHONE. The growth of the very modern system of shopping by telephone has. in the case of Messrs. Henry Milling and Co. Ltd., the well-known Grocers and Provision Dealers, necessitated the doubling of their telephone service. Messrs. Milling and Co. have recently installed a second wire for receiving orders Rod instructions thus largely obviating the ever-recurring annoyance to customers of finding the time "engaged." In view of the pressure of the Xmas Trade this will doubtless prove a great relief and a welcome convenience to Messrs. Milling's customers. Both telephone lines are included in the No. 48 which stands for "Milling" at the Central Office.—Advt.

The bankrupt stated that he commenced the business he was carrying on up till June last, thit af a travelling draper, in February last at his dwelling house. Ho had no capital. Prior to that the .bamkrupt had been engaged in 1874 in the travelling drapery business, sometimes on his own account and sometimes as an employee.
