Stock ranges from ss. to £5 per pair.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. The Assessment of Wlnwick Asylum.

A meeting of the Warrington Union Assessment committee was held at the Workhouse, yesterday morning, Hr. J. Billinge presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. A. J. Jolley, W. Gill, R. Bolton, W. Furness, A. Brooks, A. Bottomley (clerk), rt. R. Meacock (valuer),W. H. Woodcock (assistant overseer for Warrington), C. Cola (assistant overseer for Newton), and E. C. Cooper (assistant overseer for Win wick). Mr. Francomb (solicitor) appeared for the Lancashire Asylums Board with regard to the assess- ment of the County Asylum, Winwick, at £8,500 gross and £,7,225 ratable. Be stated that lie was not present as an objector in the strictest sense of the term but more as an inquirer into the principles on which the assessment was made. If the Board got that information it would probably satisfy them.—lt was stated that since the last assessment in 1902 new buildings bad been erected or completed at a cost of f-23,000. The alterations included the completion of the churches and chapels. the erection of cottages and piggeries,and improved Winwick Hall buildings.—The Clerk explained that at the last meeting of the County Rating Committee in Manchester the question was considered and the committee agreed that £1,500 would be a reasonable sum to add to the assessment. The overseers accordingly made the increase—Mr. Francomb said he wished to know on what principle the Asylum as a whole was assessed, how much was allocated to land, and the principle on which the large increase in respect of the new buildings had boon arrived at.—The Clerk said there was no basis. The

County Pates Committee thought £1,500 would be a reasonable sum.—Eventually the committee decided to make no change pending a complete revaluation of the Asylum by their own valuer.—The Chairman said the probability wss that the assessment as it stood was a reasonable one. When the old valultion was arrived at some of the buildings were not in occupa- tion but they were occupied now.

Women's Suffrage Society. The first of a series of monthly " At Homes," was held last evening, over Messrs. Wadsworth's shop, Sankey street. Mrs. J. A. Ransorne. M.A.., and Miss Corney read papers on "Women's Suffrage," which were followed by an interesting discussion. Refresh- ments were partaken of at the Winmarlegh Cafe. National Union of Clerks. In these days when the old adage that union is strength is seen to be more and more put int) practice with beneficent results, it is not surprising that the immense number of clerks who are spread over the country should associate for the purposes of mutual aid and snpport. The headquarters of the National Union of Clerks is in London, the central office being ' in Clerkenwell-road, but the institution has numerous branch secretaries at work, the Warrington official being Mr. W. Palma, of 289, Lovely-lane. The union offers to its members unemployed benefits, sick benefits, death benefits,insurance on better terms than can be got elsewhere, and, finally, the advantages of an unemployed bureau are thrown in. A bright and informative monthly journal called "The Clerk" is , issued by the union at the popular price of a penny by means of which the policy and aims of the large body of its members are voiced and pressed forward. Commission Agent Falls. At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court, yesterday, before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, James Clayton, corn ' mission agent, Laurel Bank, Weaste-lane, Thelwall, appeared for his public examination.—The liabilities were estimated at F... 89 15s. lid. and the assets amounted to 21 10s. leaving a deficiency of 68 ss. 11d.—Bankrupt stated that he commenced business as agent for an assurance company in November, 1898, since which date he had acted as agent for var:ous societies in the Warrington district. He attributed his insolvency to interest on loans, law costs and insufficient income. The Official Receiver in his observations stated that the explanation of the bank- rupt's position appeared to be inability to make a sufficiently lucrative c :nnection. —The examination was closed. ICoal Dealer's Bankruptcy. _ .

. - At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court, yesterday, before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, Francis James Dolan, coal dealer, carrying on business at 2, Antrobus-street, Warrington, appeared for his public examination. Mr. Browne represented the bankrupt. The state- ment of affairs showed liabilities estimated at £217 19s. 10d. and assets of £22 9s. od., leaving a deficiency of £195 10s. 4d.. Bankrupt stated that he commenced business as a coal dealer in September, 1905, with £5O cash capital. In November, 1900, he was joined as a partner by his brother Dennis and the business was carried on until September, 1907, as D. and F. Dolan, when a dissolution was effected, the assets being divided and the liability being taken over by each partter in certain proportion. The bankrupt attributed his insolvency to bad debts, loss on lion-es, decrease in turnover this year owing to competition. Questioned by 11r. 3. Grant Gibson, official receiver, the bankrupt stated that at the time of the dissolution of partnership he believed the business was solvent. Asked how be explained the deficiency having arisen since then, be said he had been unlucky in dealing in horses and the Co-operative Society had taken a llt of his best customers—The Official Receiver : Was it not going outside the limits of your business to go over to Ireland to buy horses on chance of selling them at increased prices in England ? —Bankrupt : Yes. The Official Receiver: It really was a bit of a gamble? —Bankrupt: Yes. —rhe bankrupt stated that altogether he had bought and re-sold forty horses. He was not an expert in horses, and could not even tell their age.— In answer to Mr. Browne, bankrupt said his takings went down from .£2O to £9 a week owing to the competi- tion of the Co-operative Society. —The examination was closed.

THE GUARDIANS' PRIVATE FUND.

WHERE THE MONEY HAS GONE. - A Remarkable Document.

j Great interest and speculation have been aroused in the town by the discussions at the Board of Guardians moeting,s with regard to the mysterious Private or j Nemo Fund. It has already been openly stated that the Fund is maintained by the bank interest accruing on the Warrington Union General Account, and that it has been in existence since before 1882. In that year a Local Government Board Auditor objected to the Fund and pronounced the payments from it to be illegal. The Guardians contended that they were in order, and though the matter was referred to the Local Government Board, no further action was taken or any protest made until this year, when the Board's auditor again objected. As a result of the discussions which have ensued the Guardians have recently decided to close the Nemo Fund and apply bank interest to the pup )ses of the Union in the ordinary way. A statement of receipts and expenditure under the Nemo Fund for the five years ending June, 1908, has been prepared and supplied confidentially to each member of the Board of Guardians. Having been privileged to see a copy of this statement, we feel convinced that it is in the public interest that the details should be published. The policy of secrecy in a matter which concerns the expenditure of public money only engenders suspicion, and it is better that the ratepayers should, like the Guardians their servants, have the information before them.

The particulars of expenditure in the five years mentioned are so voluminous that we have only appended those for the first, second and fifth years. The items possess a great similarity and many recur with frequency. Our extract gives a fair idea of the purposes for which the Netno Fund has been used. PRIVATE FUND ACCOUNT. _

This is the first of the confidential papers and is in the nature of a balance sheet of the Fund for the five years ending June 1908. On the income side are the half-yearly credits for bank interest, amounting to £550 17*. for the five years. On the expenditure side are the particulars of cheques drawn on the Fund, with the date, name of drawee, and amount in each case. The cheques have nearly all been drawn by officials of the Board, and total £ll5 2s. Id., leaving a balance of £lll 11s. 11d. GARDEN SUBURB SEWERAGE The ,Ceerste read a letter from Mr. Smith, Clerk to Sankey Parish Council, intimating that the Council had considered the offer from the •Penketh Parish Council re the use of Heath Road sewer. Mr. BROOKS : When Sankey get a sewerage scheme, will they allow the Garden Suburb to bo exempt from sewerage rate Mr. FURNESS : No, that is another thing altogether. The CLERK said the Council had no power to make the Garden Suburb Company undertake the responsi- bility whicn really rested upon the Council. The SURYEYoR raid the total cost of laying the pipes and connecting the sewer would be £57 10s. The houses were now almost completed so that the sewer might he required at any time. Mr. BROOKS suggested that when the work had been done it would be a matter for the Great Sankey Parish Council to negotiate with the Garden Suburbs Company as to payment. WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The await-1g of the Garden Suburb.

QISEASE GERMS ENTER NAKED WOUNDS,

Eczema Breaks out on Scalded Arm.

A Mass of Sores That Zam-Bak Alone Could End.

,:

I

I

I

An open cut or sore is like a msgaet for attracting disease germs, and these minute living creatures, unless speedily checked, will poison the fleshy tissues and work serious havoc. In this way Miss Mary Kerr of New Buildings, Cope Street, Wolverhampton, got itching, burning eczema from a scalded arm. Zara- Duk, however. quickly destroyed the germs and restored Miss Kerr's skin to health. In conversation with a local reporter, Miss Kerr said: —"Twelve months ago, whilst working as a core-maker in a Manchester foundry, I scalded my left arrn through tumbling with a pan of boiling viater. Later, as the injury _seemed to be doing well I left oil the bandages, exposing the raw flesh. The skin afterwards began to itch and burn until I could scarcely bear the pain and irritation. Small watery blisters formed and spread up the arm. "The doctor s,tid it was eczema. He was of opinion that I had &tiler got some dirt in the wound or after- wards eaught eczema through us;ng strange towels. Seven weeks later, the disease was worse than ever, and spread over my hand and arm to the elbow as if the germs had entered right into my system. Nothing that the doctor could do brought any relief and the sore got deeper and more obstinate.

"I tried ordinary ointments, but the arm got worse still. I conld scircely sleep for the pain, and feared the disease spreading all over me. "After I had eight months of agotiv, one of the girls at work told me what wonderful benefit her father got from Zatn-Buk for a had skin disease. I sent for a box of Zam-Buk, which I smeared over the sores and found very soothing. After applying this rich balm I bandaged up with clean lint or rag. When I removed the bandages I was surprised at the improrement Zant-Ruk had brought. The sores were leas angry, and I saw that Zara-Buk was thawing away the had mattn.

" I continued the applications of Zam-Bnk night and morning, and my arm lt?aled beautifully. First the discharge stopped ; then the sores scabbed over and dried up; finally falling away and leaving a nice smooth skin. lkfy cure has caused quite a sensa- tion amongst my fellow-workers, wh% all knew what a shocking arm I had before I had used Zam-Buk. We are never without Zam-Buk now."

- The Examiner, Saturday December 5, 1908

KNOTSFORD DIVISION LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

Resignation of Mr. Joseph Smith.

Liberals of the knutsford Division will hear with regret of the resignation of Mr. Joseph Smith, the Liberal Ragistration Agent. Mr. Smith has always. combined political enthusiasm with hard work,and an unfailing geniality which has won him many friends even in the enemies' camp. Mr. Smith finds that advancing years make him less able to carry on the work of such an extensive division as that of Knutsford, particularly in regard to the organising and attending of evening tfieetings in remote villages, and he has been reluctantly compelled to resign his position. Mr. Smith's official connection with Liberalism dates back to 49 years ago, when he took an active part in the 1853 Campaign in Manchester. This was the last election at which there was open voting. He first became connected with Cheshire ILiberalism in 1832' when he supported Mr. 1. J. Leadam ' in the Altrincharn Division, and worked for him in the election of 1885, and the by.electlon of 1886. In 1887 Mr. Smith removed to Knutsford where his services were welcomed by the local Liberals, and he was 53013 appointed hon, treasurer to the committee, a position which he held until his appointment as Secretary of the-Liberal Association,and Registration Agent, 14 years ago. When he undertook the work Mr. Smith found the organization and register in a chaotic condition. The register was crammed nth " deads," " dnubtfuls " and duplicates," and it was only through untiring effort that Mr. Smith was able to get it in order. His reward came in 1006 when Mr. A. J. King wrested the seat from the Conservative party, and because the first Liberal member for the constituency. Since, then, the Liberal position in the division has been strengthened and fortified in every direction by the efforts of the Member and Mr. Smith and there is every hope that the seat will be held for the cause of progress when the next trial of strength comes round. The Stockton Heath Liberal Association has unanimously passed a resolution expressing regret at Mr. Smith's retirement, and their high apprecia- tion of his great services to Liberalism in the Dia ision, and wishing him many happy, years of comfort in the future.

Mr- H. P. Greg, chairman of the Knntsford Division Liberal Assc;iation, paid a high tribute to Mr. Smith's fourteen years' set 'rice as Agent and Secre- tary, at a meeting at Wilmslow the other evening. He said Mr. Smith would retire at the end of the year with the respect and esteem of every Liberal in the Division. (Applause.) He msidered they owed a deep debt of gratitule to Mr. Smith for the immense amount of work, of time, and of energy he had put into the cause. (Applause.) 'They wvuld part wlth his services with the greatest regret, and he trusted that Mr. Smith would continue to take a very earnest interest in Liberr'isna in the Knntsford Division.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BACKHAUS -ELLA RUSSELL CONCERT'

The concert in Parr Hall on Monday promoted by Mr. R. H. Pearson evidenced the gl'omiug. popularity of Herr Backhaus as a piano exponent. But for a somewhat sparse attendance in the first reserved seats, the building was nicely filled. Backhaus himself was in 6ne form and played in the superb manner only so accomplished an artiste can when the circumstances are entirely favourable. Of Backhaus's technique one stands in wondering admiration, and it is clearly seen that it is just the perfection of it in his playing that leaves him free to devote himself to the disclosure of the deeper meanings of the compositions he sets him- self to interpret. Beethoven, Chopin and Liszt were the composers whose works occupied Backhaus's attention, although in response to tumultuous encores there were added it-ms by Schumann, Handel and Mendelssohn. In this galaxy scope was of course found for almost all styles, warmth .and breadth alternating with subtle nuances of expression and tone gradations ranging in a marvellous way from a whispered pianissimo to an overwhelming fortissimo. It is, however, as an exponent of Chopin that Backhaus excels, and if one were asked to instance the high-water mark I of Backhatis's capacity to deal with the works of this II entrancing composer, the " Bercease" would probably !come to mind most prominently. But Liszt's "La Campanella " nedoubtedly most completely captivated the pianist's audience as it also demonstrated most unmistakably that marvellous perfection of technique on the part of Backhaus to which we have already alluded. Mme. Ella Russell's voice has not escaped time's , ravares though her use of it is as artistic as ever. The Tannhauser excerpt" Elizabeth's prayer and greet- ing "is too heavy for concert singing and suffers too much by severance from its stage environment to make it possible to hold an audience's attention when rendered in this way. Her best item was the " Ave Maria " (Janotha) which she gave with deep devotional feeling. A most skilful and tasteful accompanist was found in Mr. R. J. Forbes.

CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

(By PEDo).

WARRINGTOINT A.C. had a very interesting run on Saturday last. The occasion was the first contsst in connection with the Point Competition. Teams were selected by Ti. K. Jepson (captain and club champion) and A. Holbrook (sub-capt-..in and winner of Point Competion last season.) The race was run over nearly seven miles of good country. Right from the start Holbrook took the lege. and although repeatedly challenged he maintained his position throughout, and was first man home. about 50 yards in front of Antrobus who is showing mach improveliform this season. 50 yards farther away Ford was third and is apparently intent on improving on his last season's display which was exceptional for a beginner. J. Parsonage and Jepson are not at their best yet and the pair were divided by a very promising novice in J. George. A. Siddall gained the seventh position after a good dust up with A. Millington, the pair finishing the last 120 yards at sprinting pace; 8.) yards away flowitt was ninth and put dawn a good performance for a youth. At 150 yards off a triangular duel was taking place between J. Stewart, J. Dakev and J. Litberland neither of whom bad anything to spare when finishing close together in the order given. W. Litherland and R. Whittaker were most prominent of the others Watmongh, Bennett, Beswick, Taylin and Marshall did not compete. Result: Sub-Captain's team : H. Holbrook 1, A. Ford 3, J. Parsonage 4, S. Hewitt 9, J. Stewart 10, J. Oakes 1t— Total 33. Captain's team : W. Antrobus 2. J. George 5. H. K. Jepson 6, A. Siddall 7, A. Millington 8, W. Litherland 13—Total 41. Point Competition :—H. Holbrook 10, W. Antrobus 9, A. Ford 8, J. Parsonage 7. J. George 6, H. K. Jepson 5, A. Siddall 4, A. Millington 3, S. Hewitt 2, J. Stewart I. To-day's run is from the " Red Lion "at Moore, 2 p.m. train from Bank Quay to Daresbnry. Next Satur- day the fixture is Crewe Harriers at Crewe (Oak Farm Hotel), 1.46 train from Bank Quay. Complete arrange- ments have been made for the Anneal Dance or:the 11th inst.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

CLAIM FOR SICK PAY. STRONG CRITICISM OF AN INSURANCE SOCIETY. At Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday last John -Hughes, of Crow-lane, Earlestown, sued the One and All Sickness and Accident Association, which. his offices in London, for £l2, sick pay which he alleged was due to him as a memt et. of the Society.—Mr. Rigby of Colborne, appeared for the plaintiff. The defendant company was rot represented, but the Justices' Clerk (Mr. Taylor) said he had received a letter that morning from a solicitor named Whittingham, who said he had rsceived instructions to defend, but declined to do so. —Mr. Rigby then said the character of the Association was pretty well-known. ST. HELENS.

AN INCORRIGIBLE ROGUE. ST. HELENS HUSBAND'S DESERIION.

A somewhat unusual course was taken at St. Helens on Monday in the case of Michael Kennedy, a middle- aged glassrnaker, who was charged with deserting his 'wife and family.—Mr. Marsh, relieving officer said since January last it has cost the Guardians £3O 15s. to maintain this man's wife and family, and he had not done anything for them.—Superintendent Thompson I said the man's wife and children had been continuously chargeable to the Guardians since January 21st, 1903, and the total cost had bran over £230. He had been sent to gaol twice from that court. He asked the bench to pass the severest possible sentence they could.— The Clerk : The severest possible penalty the bench can inflict is three months' hard labour, but you can apply for his committal to the quarter sessions as an incorrigible rogue.—The magistrates decided to send Kennedy to the quarter sessions, and ordered that in the meantime he should be kept to hard labour.

BIG DRINK OF LAUDANIM.

George Lewis, stoker, of College-street, St. Helens, was brought before the St. Helens magistrates on Tues- day, on a .barge of attempted suicide.—The Chief/Con- stable said Lewis was a widower with eight children, and he had taken a large quantity of laudanum, which he had secured at a shop in the town on the statement that he wanted it for toothache. It was alleged that he had been keeping company with a young women and that they had a qiarrel on Monday. The man's little ' boy raised the alarm and Police-constable Griffiths and Dr. Fox was called in. Several emetics were adminis- tered, and six policemen held him while the doctors administered the stomach pump. Then he walked about for seventeen hours until the time for the court to sit. They had the greatest difficulty in preventing the man falling asleep for ever.—Le wis said he had not quarrelled with any young vvomao.—Lowis was remqnded for a week.

Mr DEAR CHILDREN,

I am very pleased indeed with the efforts of the boys in coming to the front with their essays on football. I am glad to find that they are so enthusiastic over their games. Un- doubtedly, football is the favourite with my nephews. Well, boys, I am pleased to find so many of you have such a strong liking for games. We want our boys to be manly, and not afraid of a little outdoor life during the winter months. . _

The girls, evidently, are not going to be beaten in the interest shown in the Christmas competitions.

THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE CHRIST- MAS OF A VERY POOR CHILD AND THAT

OF A RICH CHILD.

OFO-11,-iii-e—T)oor child has not such good_ clothes as the rich child, and cannot play with such nice toys: Second, poor children sometimes, at Christmas, have to stay out, in the cold, while rich children can play..round a ince fire ud a large Christmas tree. On Christ- mas Eve rich children have rich and costly toys to put into their stockings, and if the poor children have drunken parents they are very often sent out starving in the cold weather to beg. If rich children see some of their friends who are very poor they very often scoff and jeer at them because of their old clothes, but sometimes the rich children become poor them- selves, and then they know what it is to be scoffed and jeered at. When a Man has plenty of money he has many friends, but when his money fs gone his friends go too.

MAY SHAKESPEARE. -4.-

THE CHRISTMAS OF A POOR CHILD AND THE CHRISTMAS OF A RICH CHILD.

A rich child's Christmas is a Christmas full , of happiness and fun, while a "Icor child hardly knows what the words mean. Of course, Christ- mas is a happy time. A few days before Christ- mas the rich lads and las3ies are employed in cending out invitation cards, asking their friends to their parties. They have plenty to eat and drink, plenty of dancing, etc.; while the poor children are often singing, and beg- ging to get a crust for dinner. The rich have plenty Of poultry and wines, and often have too merry a time; while the poor strive to get a cup of tea. Some very poor children walk the streets, barefooted, bareheaded, and hungry; no home have they got in which to play games and dance to music. They are, in most cases, in this position through a drunkard father or mother. All are not. so situated; some have lost their parents. We are pleased. to learn kind friends have made homes for such as these. PLOICENCE E. DIRK. Dear Uncle ,/sTed,—l have been reading your letter every week, and I would like to be a member of the "Children's Corner." Please will you send me a pocket mirror, if you have any left? I hope to see my name in print next week with the others. I am writing a short tale, and I hope to see it in the paper. It is called "The Man and the Dog." In one of the crowded streets 9f New York a beggar is in the habit of taking his stand every day and appealing to the charity of the passers- by. By means of a short ,string he holds a dog, around the neck of which a card is fastened, "I am blind." A very kind old gentleman, who had been in the habit of dropping a penny into the beggar's hat, passed rapidly one morning without doing so. ,Instantly the beggar rushed after him, and asked for the penny to be given as usual. The gentleman, turning in surprise, said, "Why, I thought you were blind?" "Oh, no, sir," was the cool reply, "it is the ' dog that is blind." BESSIE PICKTON.

WHICH I PREFER-FOOTBALL OR CRICKET.

The game I prefer of the two, and the one which is indulged in most, is football. Cricket is the game which we play in summer, and really we do not need games then, as we can keeP ourselves hot enough. We only play that game for the sport we get out of it and to pass away the time. Football is a game that cannot be played in summer, as the weather would be too hot. Football is the best winter's pastime yet invented. There is a lot of running attached to football, which makes our legs firm and strong. At cricket we have only got a few chances to show our knowleclize and skill of the game, but at foot- ball we are constantly having chances to put the ball into thegoal. The goalkeeper has rather a cold time of it, as he is standing still most of the time. Football is a very cheap game, as it only costs about is. 6d. for a ball and about 105. 6(1. for posts, whereas cricket costs a good bit more. - In England we have many prominent footballers. At football we ave apt to get injured, but we have to put up with that. There is a great deal of sport attached to football, kicking the ball one to ^C.nothtr, which I like. HARRY Cops'.

CRICKET AND FOOTBALL. WHICH I PREFER, AND WHY.

Cricket is the national game of England. It is played by two sides' generally of eleven players each. At a distance of twenty-two yards apart, the wickets, that is, three stumps, are pitched. On the top of these stumps are transverse pieces of wood, called bails. When it. , has been decided which side is to bat first, the game begins. The batsmen take their Places, one at each. wicket, while the captain of the opposite side places his men in different parts of the field. One bowls from behind the bowling-crease at one wicket, and endeavours to hit with the ball the stumps at the other end. This the batsman trys to prevent by hit- ting the ball with his bat. Should he hit the ball a sufficient distance, the two batsmen try- to cross from one wicket to the other before the fielders get the ball back to .the wicket. This is called a run,iand is put down to.the credit of the striker. Should one 'of them fail to reach his ground before one of the opposite side can knock the bails off, he is out. A batsman ccai also be out by any of the fielders catching a ball hit by him before it touches the ground, or by the bowler knocking the bails off his stumps, or by placing himself In such a posi- tion that the ball cannot hit the wicket. When all the players of one side are out, the other side bate, while their opponents take then; places in the field. The game is won by the ' side that scores the most runs. Cricket is sup- posed to be a development of the old English game of club-ball. The cricket season lasts from May 1 to August 31. Football is the chief of the outdoor winter games. This, like cricket, is played by two parties. It is played under two sets of rules, viz., the Football Association and the Rugby. By the former the parties play eleven a-side, and by the latter fifteen. In the former also the ball is round, and must. only be touched by the feet except the cne, that person that keeps goal. Also the goal-posts, which are at each end of the field, have the crossbar on top, while in the Rugby it is lower down. The object of both parties is to get the ball under the bar, ,and into the net in the former, and in the latter the players, all of whom can use any part of their bodies with the ball, try to force or drive the ball over the tar. If they run the ball over the marked line a try is scored, and it is the side that scores most tries that wins the game. Rugby football is played under two sets of rules, namely, the Rugby Union and the Northern Union rules. Both rules are the came, except for the points, each giving different points for tries, dropped goals,. goals scored from tries, etc. On the field, in both the Association and Rugby football, is a referee,. who, with the aid of linesmen, keeps the game in order, and signals time and half-time. Asso- ciation football is played in the Midlands, the North of England, and in Scotland chiefly; and the Rugby in Wales and Yorkshire chiefly. The game that I prefer is cricket, because it exercises every muscle of the body, gives a. quick and sure eye, and is a very healthy exer- cise all round. Although I prefer cricket, foot- ball, if played properly, is also a very healthy exercise. W. SCOTT.

CRICKET AND FOOTBALL. WIFCCH GAME I PREFER, AND WHY.

Of the two 'games, cricket and football, I much prefer the latter, and no, I think, would most other boys, if they had had a black eye and a swollen nose, as I have. Cricket, in my estimation, savours too much of civilisation,' While football seems a more rugged and hardy game. The mention of the name of football seems to impress upon my mind a vague idea tif a grand and noble game, and a footballer— a real, true, English footballer—is, in my mind, to be more esteemed than a prince, or even a king; but a cricketer is, in my idea, an every- day sort of man. Cricket, too, is a very expen- sive game, and, when hitting the. ball, I feel a kind of restraint, as if I should be charged. with manslaughter next moment. A hundred years ago football was not what it is now. Thea there were about sixty or seventy boys on each side, and most boys helped with both feet and both fists too, generally. The game usually ended with a few broken legs, or arms, or perhaps heads. Rugby, too, comes of the same family as football, but when playing rugger, the fists are used instead of the feet. This, too, is a fine game—in fact, almost as good an foot- ball. When I am playing cricket I feel a sort of dread as I see that awfal ball whizzing in my direction; or, when I am batting, I have the same sort of feeling as I had when I looked into the baths for the first time.

A lady having accidentally broken her smelling-bottle. her husband, who was very petulant, said to her, "I declare, my dear, every- thing that belongs to you is more or less broken." S. RICHARDS.

Two correspondents wrote to a country editor to know, respectively, " The best way of assist- ing twins through the teething period?" and "How to rid an orchard of grasshoppers?" The editor answered, but got the names mixed, unfortunately, and the fond father was thunderstruck by this advice:—" If you are un- fortunate enough to be plagued by these un- welcome little pests, the quickest means of settling is to cover them with straw and set the straw on fire." The other man, who was troubled with grasshoppers, was equally amazed to read:—" The best method of treatment is to give them a warm bath, twice a day, and rub their gums twice or thrice with indiarubber." SIMEON RICHARDS.

POSTAL EMI) SAVINA BANK REGULNTIONS

For further particulars see "Post Moe Guide," at any Post Office. POSTAGE STAMPS.

Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post office. Price 2s.

ISLAND CORR.ESPOD ESC E.

The term "Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size.

LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... id. For every additionai 2 0z5.... Id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or oue foot in depth. POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable oa delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 31 inches in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and 1-inch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating tea post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter.

HALFPENNI PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, Fro., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. . . .

The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is id. Newspapers not registered at the General Post (Mice are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter. PARCEL POST.

The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 PP exceeding 1 lb. PP 2 lb. 0 4 fl ° lb PP 3 lb. 0 5 IP . PP 3 lb. PP 5 lb. 0 6 PP If 5 lb. PI 7 lb. 0 7 n PP 7 lb. n 8 lb. 0 8 PP 0 8 lb. PP 9 lb. 0 9 IP PI 9 lb. n 10 lb. 0 10 n „ 10 lb. 11. lb. 011 No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel is:— Greatest length... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. Gin, in its longest dimensions as much as 21t. Gin, round its thickest part ; a short parcel may be thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft. in length, it may measure as much as 31t. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid,. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving.

PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL).

Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid.

MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.)

The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a " id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 6d. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under c3rtain restrictions. • MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.)

The maximum amount for which a money order may. be issued for pittces abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. • £2, Gd. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional 2or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order oflice in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can he cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by 6d., from 6d. to £1 is. The poundage fees are :—id. up to 2s. 6d.; Id. up to 155.; Id. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain uo further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums.

TELEGRAMS (N'LAND).

The Postmaster-General pOssesses,a'l most entirely, the privilege of transmitting,cellec,.ing and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of lid., which covers 12 words, and an additional id. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph otlice, but if from a sub-culice then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of klif. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certniti cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must cost of two words, is is. MOTOR CAR AND CYCLE REGULATIONS.

Each one must be registered w ith the council of county or county borough, the fee being .21 fored motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A register a number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep alliNod when driving. Erich driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may he endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences. _ _ .

The highest speed at which a car may be drivAn on-the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions.

It is an offence under the Nictor Car Act (10:3), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway es to be dangerous to the public, hiving regard to all the circumstances of the case. _ .

A Shock for Runcorn.

1 After soundly administering the first defeat to the Oldham team, Warrington went confidently to Runcorn on Saturday, and proved themselves the first League Club capable of beating the Linnets on their own ground. There was an excellent crowd of 4,000 people, and a very keen game was witnessed. In the second half Langley, of Runcorn and O'Neill of Warrington were sent off for striking, and a few minutes later Woods, another of the Runcorn forwards, sus:ained fractured leg in making a wild kick at the ball. The game was not so rough as these regrettable incidents would suggest. The teams were : Runcorn—Hughes, back ; Unsworth, Butterworth, , Moran, and Flanagan, three-quarter backs; Jolley and ' Watton, half backs ; Walker, Ricilardson, Wood, McDonald, Langley, and Kennedy, forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Treheitrne, three-quarter backs ; Lees and Jenkins, half backs ; O'Neil, Dowell, Thomas, A. E. Boardman, P. Boardman and Cheaters, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Priestley, Salford. Touch judges, Messrs. A. Critchley (Widnes) and R. H. Jackson (St. Helens). Richardson started and Runcorn, mainly through the good work of Jolley, and the front rank, took up a strong position and all the resources of Treharne and Tilley were brought into play to deal with the danger- ous attack. The Warrington Sextette speedily retaliated and the outcome of some sterling work by, Dowell, Neill and P. Boardman resulted in tho "Wirepullers" gaining ground beyond the half-way. Further advantage was gained, when Jolley had a kick charged, and for several minutes the Runcorn goal was in great jeopardy. Jolley with a huge kick placed the game well inside the Warrington quarters. The sides continued to be well matched and neither was able to maintain the attack for any lengthy period. Another fine attempt by Jolley to got through was well met by Dickenson, who kicked and followed up and floored Moran before he could reply. Lees and Jenkins in turn tried to break through, but all to no purpose, and in some exchange kicking Tilley sent to Jolley, who, together with several of his confrerers, was Nvell placed. The Run- corn skipper responded by racing to almost half-way, where he handed on to Flanagan, who immediately threw inside to Butterworth, and the latter scored good a try, amidst deafening cheers. Jolley missed the goal. Warrington were soon on the attack after the restart from half-way, and Fish and Neill breaking away, Hughes failed to hold the ball and Lees scored a try. Thomas failed with the goal kick. Half-time : Runcorn, tone try (three points). Warrington 1 try (3 points). Turning round the game again waxed fast and both goals were menaced in turn. A penalty was also I missed by Jolley from an easy position and then came j Warrington's turn, for bold forward rushes forced matters at the Runcorn end. Fish and Tilley ; got the better of some exchange kicking with Hughes and Watton, and pressed hard Jolley punted high to Dickenson, who was stationed near the half-way line. Steadying himself for a drop-kick he sent the ball over the bar with a huge effort. Following this came some spirited play at the Warrington end, and for some , moments the visitors had an anxious time of it. The I players struggled hard and in a melee near the Runcorn line two players were sent off, Neill and Langley receiving marching orders. Near the close Rnncrn sustained a serious loss when Wood in a collision had the misfortune to sustain a fracture of the right leg. First aid was rendered, and Wood was eventully con- veyed home on the ambulance. The closing stages of the game were fought out in semi-darkness and nothing of any moment transpired. Result :—Warrington one goal one try (five points) ; Runcorn, one try (three three points).

ST. HELENS v. BRADFORD NOTHERN. At Knowsley Road, St. Helenq, before a fair attend ance. Splendid combination by Wilson and Howarth put the home line in danger,but Trenwith saved magni- ficently in front of the posts. Bradford were superior forward, and made many attempts to get through, and eventually -;t. Helens were patmlised for obstruction close in, and Downs had little difficulty in placing Bradford two points ahead. Half-Grue score :—Bradford 1 goal (2 points), St. Helens nil.—Downs restarted the game for Bradford, and eventually Trenwith gave to Briars, who ran half the length of the field and scored under the posts. " Matt " Creevey lauded a goal. Following unproductive shots at goal by Downs and " Matt " Creevey, Briers secured and gave to Prescott, who threw out to Barton, and the latter dashed over at the corner, Creevev failed to convert. Result :—St. Helens 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points), Bradford 1 goal (2 points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON "A " RUNCORN "A."—These teams tried conclusions at Warrington on Saturday before a good attendance. The home side was a particularly strong one. After the opening exchanges the locals were the first to take up an attracting position, but no impression was made on the visitors' defence, although Landor was unfortunate enough to be held up when over the line. The home team rushed play to the visitors' " 25," where Lambert made an unsuccessful attempt to drop a goal. Jordan, however, by smart following up, managed to touch down before the full- back could make the ball dead, and Gibbs added the goal. Mclntyre was troubled by several rushes on the part of the Runcorn forwards, but managed to keep his lines intact. A quick change saw the hotue team hover- ing near their opponents' line, and after some scra tub] 'ng .play Naylor got over. Gibbs being again successful. Several passing movements were initiated amongst the visiting backs by White and the home contingent were hard pres9ed to keep them out. At length faulty play by the " Wirepullers " was taken full advantage of end the visitors were five points up. A couple of free kicks to the "Linnets" were unproductive and then the home vanguard took matters in their own bands—and feet—and Felton forced himself over, to goal resulting. At half-time Warrington were leading by 13 points to five. For a goodly portion of the second half Runcorn had the advantage so far as play was concerned, but the locals were the first to gather points, Bradshaw doing the trick after good work by Lambert. Mclntyre kicked a grand goal from near tie touch-line. Okell got well away, but being unsupported the effort came to nought. The home backs once more got going and Bradshaw crossed again, the kick failing. Just before the close, White, the visiting half, who had played a brilliant game, was injured and had to be carried oil. Final : Warrington "A," three goals Pve tries (21 points); Runcorn "A," one goal one try (five points).

Latchford 3, Orford 2. Esrlestown St. John's Res. 1, Thelwall United 0. Rylands Res. 3. Whitecross Res. 3. SALVATION ARMY LECTURE.

An interesting limelight lecture on "Emigration " was given by Adjutant Pinchen (of London) at the Salvation Army Hall, on Saturday evening. The Adjutant said that during last winter certain editors had published letters in their papers against emigration to Canada. lie believed most of the letters were untruths. The Salvation Army helped hard-working people who were unable to pay their fare to emigrate and also undertook to find them situations. The emigrants, however, refunded the money and a small interest. The Army had already sent to Canada 40,000 of whom only one per cent were deported,either through ill- • health or undesirability, which vouched for the good , work carried on throughout the country by the Salva- tion Army. The Canadian Government wanted men who could work, and not men who did not want work. ' It had representatives all about the country and if they ' saw a man who appeared to be an undesirable be was ' picked up and landed again in Liverpool, providing he I ' came from this country. Only a few weeks ago, 1,000 I of this class of people were landed at Liverpool. He ! gave a true instance of one man who went out to Canada ' taking with him his wife and three children. After a while the man went away and left his family. The , Salvation Army took up the case, had the women and I children convoyed to England and found a place for , them in the Salvation Army home. The lecturer strongly ad,vised the young men not to emigrate during the winter ; months as work was so scarce during that time. He also advised men who had good situations in this country not to leave them to emigrate. Ile knew a man who , was earning £3 a week in this country, and who went out to Canada. After a few months time he returned ' grumbling because he could only earn £4 a week. lie ladvised would-be emigrants to go out some time about , February. Good, strong, healthy men were wanted. ! There was millions of acres of land in Canada covered ' with shrub and forest waiting for the axe of the settler " to clear away and till the land. Men could also earn ! good money working on the farms. The lecturer stated that many people bad been criticising the good work of the 'Salvation Army, in sending the people out to Canada. It was no disgrace to be sent out to Canada by the Army. He explained that many poor people unable to get work in this country were sent out there and are now doing well. Some men who were living I in large town's before emigrating, went to work on the farms away from all the hurry and bustle of the large towns, after a few! months they get tired of it and longed for some busy centre. They left a good situation, went to Winnipeg, or some other large town, and could not get work, and so they came home dis- gusted with the land. Canada was thirty times the v size of the United Kingdom, and there were 80 millions of acres of wheat-growing lands. Canada was practically an English populated country about 00 per cent. being English. The. climate during the winter was very cold but dry and healthy. Fifty per cent. of the population were farmers. The lecturer pointed on't the absurdity of people when emigrating taking revolvers and rifles with them for personal safety. He stated that he bad seen a pawnshop out there stock full of revolvers and rifles which people had pawned finding no use for them. The Salvation Army had undertaken to send another contingent of emigrants out in the early part of next 3ear. The money received after- wards from these would be utilized to send others out and anyone requiring particulars, should correspond with the Salvation Army Emigration Depot, 176 A, London-road, Liverpool. He also stated that domestic servants were much wanted in Canada. Excellent wages were paid. Views were shown of the places of call of the ocean liners, and of the locomotives belonging to the Grand Trunk railway, the Canadian Pacific rail- ways and others. Portraits of prominent members of the Salvation Army were received with applause.

Orford Old Boys' Association. The sixth annual dinner of the members of the Orford Old Boys' Association was held in the Orford School on Friday evening, when about 50 " old boys" sat down to an excellent repast, catered for by the Atkicson's Cafe Co., Ltd. The annual meeting took place immediately after dinner, when the following officials were again re-elected - Mr. Cann, the school- master at Orford, prtsident ; Councillor T. S. Steel, hon. treasurer, and Mr. Thomas Reddish, hon. secretary. There was some keen Toting over the election Of the committee, and subsequently the following were elected —Messrs. R. Andrews, S. H. Antrobus, Walter Povey, W. J. Woods, Thomas Williams, J. EL Whitham, George Iligham, G. H. W. Steel, George Ball, William Attwell, Thomas Attwel and J. Willett. -A number of toasts were heartily honoure3.—Songs were given by Mr. T. Attwell, who earned applauso for his rendering of "Fighting fo the good old country" and other songs. Mr. Andrews was encored for his singing of "The powder monkey." Mr. J. Emson contributed some humorous selections and Mr. Reddish sang " Tho boys of the Old Brigade." Mr. attnes was the accompanist.

-Mr. BIRRELL at WARRINGTON

• Striking Speech on Irish Affairs, Licensing,

and Education.

Mr. CROSFIELD and the GOVERNMENT ACHIEVEMENTS.

modified and as some of them thought weakened, it was sent to the House of Lords, conveyed by the prayers of everyone who loved his fellow men, freighted with the flickering hopes of all social reformers, containing the promise of salvation to innocent and suffer- ing children in wrecked and ruined homes, carried by immense majorities of the representatives of the people that Bill, which they believed to be the founda- tion stone of the new Jersualem—(cheers)—was sent to the House of Lords and received with scorn and con- tempt and trampled upon, by those who put, as they always put, money before men, wealth before the common weal, property before the public good, class before the nation, the kingdom of darkness before the division of light. (Cheers.) Now, what was to be done? (A voice: "Down with them.") For hundreds of years the yoke of this wretched oligarchy had been on the necks of the people and he said unhesitatingly, as a rank and file man, that unless the Government were determined to decide at once drastically to deal with the question, there would be a wave of bitter disappointment and dismay throughout the ranks of the Liberal party. (Cheers.) The great moral fountains were broken up, the floods were rising, the rains of outraged justice, the winds of righteous indignation were beating upon that House and it would fall—(cheers)—and great would be the fall of it —(cheers)--because it was built upon the sands of selfishness, greed and wrong. (Cheers.) The resolution was then put to the meeting and carried with enthusiasm.

Warrington was on Saturday honoured with a visit from the Right Hoe. Augustine Birrell, K.C., Chief Secretary for Ireland, who came to speak at a great Liberal demonstration in the Parr Hall. The occasion was a memorable one in the local history of the party and the meeting proved to be one of the largest and most enthasiastic ever held in the town. The hall was filled to overflowing, and Mr. Birrell's magnificent speech was listened to with deep interest and manifest approval. Like other Cabinet Ministers, Mr. Birrell is frequently the object of unwelcome attentions on the part of the suffragettes, but so elaborate were the precautions taken on Saturday that there was not a single" Votes for Women" interruption from the beginning to the end of the meeting. Only a limited number of ladies' tickets were issued to applicants who had been " gnaranteed " by local i Liberals and without a ticket t was impossible for a member of the fair sex to gain admission. Two suffragettes came to the town with the object of prosecuting their campaign and probably of making things lively for Mr. Birrell, but they gave up the attempt to get into the hall and contented themselves with holding an open air meeting near the Post Office. It speaks volumes for the efficient organisation of the demonstration that everything passed off so peaceably and praise is due to Mr. J. Howes, jun., the Liberal agent, for his valuable services in th s direction. The police arrangements personally supervised by the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) were also excellent. The hall had been brightly decorated for the occa- sion. Over the platform was a streamer bearing the words " Crosfield ", and "Welcome to Ireland's Secretary," together with the Union Jack and the green flag of Old Ireland." The watch-words of the party were much in evidence, the mottoes displayed including : " Our Trust is in the people," Remember old age pensions," "Do Justice to Yourselves and stick to Free Trade," "Peers or People: Who shall rule ? " and "Free Trade with all the world." Before the meeting began Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., gave a choice programme of organ music. The appearance on the platform of Mr. Birrell, the Borough Member, and the local leaders of the party was the signal for enthusiastic cheering which was renewed again and again. Mr. C. E. Parker (President of the Liberal Association) presided, and was supported by the Right I Hon. Augustine Birrell, K.C. M.P., Mr. A. H Crosfield, M.P. and Mrs. Crosfield, Mr. G. Harwood, M.P., Mr. A. J. King, MP., the Revs. F. E. Evans (Vicar of Appleton)—, C. Harvey Cook, H. Wall, and R. E. Letheren, Aldermen Pierpoint, Tinni ,n, Burton, and Ellison ; Councillors Wilkinson, Dickenson, Locker, Peacock, Evans, Hawtlsorn, and Skelland ; Messrs. James Dodds, E. J. Guest, J. Upson, W. Madden, W. Furness, J. Morton, S. Wright, J. E. Richardson, G. Wallington, G. Walker, W. Clegg, W. Haigh, W. Hewitt, W. Bibby, A. H. Rathbone, Longland, Joseph Wilkinson, R. Long, T. Barnes, E. J. Mitchell, A. Perris, J. F. Awbery, J. Howes, junr., and many others. Telegrams expressing regret at being unable to attend were received from Mr. and Mrs. Ziba Armitage and Mr. Henry Roberts. LICENSING BILL SUPPORTED. The efIAIRMAN moved : "That this meeting thanks the Government for its legislative achievements, • expresses its unabated confidence in its general policy, and especially thanks it for its brave, wise, and states- manlike Bill of licensing reform. (Cheers.) It regrets • and resents the unconstitutional action of the Lords at Lansdowne House in deciding to reject the Bill before • It had been discussed by the House of Lords, and urges upon the Government not to waver in its determination to secure for the nation possession of the State created monopoly and effective popular con- trol of a trade which must be restrained in the interests of the material and moral well-being of the people." (Cheers.) Mr. Parker said he was not so much concerned about the particular way in which the Licensin,, Bill bad been done to death as in the competency of the House of Lords to act as a . body which was free to reject or accept any legislation that was passed by the representatives of the people. It seemed strange that the fate of the Bill should he sealed in a private house. (Hear, hear.) They had the House of Commons and the House of Lords, and now they had a third house—the House of Lansdowne. (Laughtd.) The country was disposed to question • whether the hereditary principle was not outgrown. There were 500 Peers in the House of Lords whose only title to sit there was that they were the sons of their fathers. The question of not only the hereditary A principle of the Home of Lords, but the environment an which the Lords were brought up—the way in which their life and association was cut off from the life of , the nation—made them a body not competent to be the final authority. (Applause.) The constitution of " the House of Lords was as clearly irrational as its power was clearly excessive. He had been reading tile debate in the House of Lords over the Bill that had only just been killed. The speeches were more of funeral orations, because the issue was decided before the Bill went to the House of Lords. As one who was 2 not associated with the Church of England, he wished to express his appreciation of the bishops who had made such a brave stand in connection with this Bill. Mr. Parker quoted the follow.ing telling passages from the speech of Lord Rosebery in the House of Lords, in which he referred to the influence of the trade in public .. life :—"lt interferes at every point and in every fibre • with your political and municipal life.—(Cheers.) It poisons political life, because the persons engaged in the trade when it comes to an election, ask themselves, not what is best for the Empire or best for the public, but what is best for the trade —(Cheers.) No one can have been engaged in municipal life without seeing that candidates were chosen, not with reference to their character or to their purity, but entirely in deference to their subservience to the trade.—(Cheers.) It is in the highest public interest, then, that this trade shou'd • be brought within measurable size and within the limit of control by the Sta•e." In conclusion Mr. Parker said it was for the people of Warrington to judge whether in the future of the borough the danger wh.ch Lord Rosehery spoke threatened their municipal life. If it did their answer must be at the following booth. • (Cheers.)

Algal said

THE GUEST OF THE EVENING. The CH IIRM X.N in introducing the guest of the evening, said the Liberal party honoured and liked Mr. Birrell—in fact he might say they loved him. The chief of the Irish Parliamentary party (Mr. John Redmond) said the other day that Mr. Birrell was the best Irish Secretary they bad ever had. (Cheers.) They were proud of Mr. Birrell and of the useful work which he was doing and still more proud that he had come to Warrington to address them. (Cheers).

_ MR. BIRRELL'S SPEECH. EMPHATIC HOME RIME DECLARATION. GOVERNMENT AND THE LORDS.

Mr. Bruttut., who W 9.4 received with loud and pro- longed cheering, said :—Mr. Chairman, Ladies and Gentlemen,—When my good friend Mr. Crosfield asked me some months ago to come to Warrington in order to speak to you I readily assented to do so at the earliest possible opportunity, for indeed, to tell the honest truth, I felt that I was somewhat overdue in Warring- ton. (Laughter.) Because it is forty years since I was in a political meeting in Warrington. (Laughter.) The occasion was no mean one. The speaker of the evening was Mr. Gladstone—(cheers)—who was then seeking the suffrages of South-West Lancashire. I think some young men in the gallery laughed rather heartily when I said forty years ago. They seemed to think that it was hardly possible that anyone should be as old as that, but I cannot help thinking there must be one or two— men of course—no ladies—(laughter)—in this hall old enough to remember that great historical fight. (Hear, hear.) Gladstone and Grenfell, Turner and Cross, were the names that were on every lip, and I was here in I Warrington to hear ---- •

Mit. UPSON'S SPEECH. Mr. J.CesoN in seconding said the Government were entitled not only to the gratitude of tho Liberal party but to the heartfelt thanks of the people of this country for the splendid way in which they had steered the ship of State across the troubled waters through which it had recently been passing. By tactful diplomacy they had brought the ship into the desired haven of a firm and established peace and by their action they had done much to mitigate that racial animosity which was born of prejudice and ignorance, suckled by fear, fed by falsehood, and nurtured by an irresponsible yellow , Press, and which was rapturously taken to the hearts of those fervid patriots who constituted the House of Lords. (Laughter.) Racial animosity was a sad reflection upon the civilzation of the twentieth century. At every point of its history the Liberal party had had to fight against the demons of mistrust, I suspicion and fear which had so often needlessly rushed I this country into all the horrors of war, which had piled up the national debt and accounted for so much of our inflated expediture upon the apparatus and munitions of war. The Government merited the thanks of the country for the great work they had done in social reform. (Cheers). They had done much for the amelioration of the lives of the people and to raise the standard of life for the workers by shortening the hours of labour in mines, by legislating for the prevention of trade disputes, extending the principles of workmen's compensation and carrying forward the movement for the protection of child life. (Cheers). The Govern- ment were deserving of praise for their earnest purpose, strenuous attempt, and largo achievement in spite of the efforts of those who sat in the gilded Chamber. (Cheers). They had done much by the Old-age Pensions Act to brighten the declining years of those nho had laboured long and who had reaped but a very unfair proportion of the fruits of their labour. They had also done something to loosen the griping grasp of greed from the throats of the people. (Cheers.) If he was able to estimate the feeling of that meeting aright,he took it that the feeling of gratitude for what had been done was swallowed up in indignation at the work which bad been going on during the past week of undoing that which the Government had attempted to do. (Cheers.) Every social reformer in the country,thoughtful men of many parties had all agreed that the most gigantic evil which threatened our social.national and municipal life to-day was that of the drink traffic. In fulfilment of . its pledges the Government introduced in the House of Commons a Bill which was satisfactory so far as it went but which did not go anything like as far as some of them thought such a measure ought to go and which probably did not contain the full mind of the Government itself. (Hear, hear.) To make them acceptable to the House of Lords, measures were whittled down and their principles watered and much legislation assumed a somewhat nebulous character. That position was absolutely intolerable. (Cheers.) The Licensing Bill was discussed throughout the land and after being thoroughly debated in the Commons,

The occasion was no mean one, nor was the issue which he then presented before the country a mean one, for it was the disestablishment and disendowment of the Irish Church. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Gladstone no sooner had the courage to proclaim that policy and announce it as his own, than he was at once, if I may quote apoet now a good deal in people's minds—John Milton—he no sooner proclaimed this policy "than straight a barb'rous noise environ'd him of owls and cuckoos, asses, apes, and dogs." (Laughter.) Violent language was hurled at his head—sacrilege, conflsoation, injustice. He was worse even than we are. We are only robbing the brewer. (Laughter.) He was accused of robbing Almighty God. That was something of the situa- tion that we were presented with then, and sitting, not in this hall, but in another great building, listening to the eloquence that fell from his lips, I little dreamed that I should ever be associated as I now am with the government of Ireland. However, here lam, after forty years, and it is comfort- ing to me to think that lam here cheering and adhering to the same policy which Mr. Gladstone then expounded as his Irish policy on the occasion when his awe- inspiring voice first fell upon my ears forty years ago in this town. who have done this thing? Well, you will find their names, those of you who are curious, in the remoter paths of biography ; you will. find their names in the division lists of the majority. And I ask you to study that division list, and compare it first with the names of those who were in the minority and ask yourselves which of the two sets of men are the men more likely to know some thing about the question which was supposed to be under discussion. In the minority you will find the bishops. A bishop is nothing to me, any more than he is to Lord Robert Cecil ; but as Lord Robert Cecil does belong to the Episcopal Church one would have thought that some decency was due from him to a bishop. (Hear, hear.) I am entirely free to judge a man by the facts of the case but who can doubt that the bishops of the Church of England, or the majority of them, are nowadays men who have laboured in their parishes and dioceses, and in a thousand- and-one ways, have come into contact with the very evil against which the bill was directed ? You will agree, therefore, that one bishop outweighs twenty peers, who have given no particular indication of caring anything for the issue at stake. Then, com- pare the majority in the House of Lords with the majority in the House of Commons. lathe majority in the House of Commons you will find ALL THE LABOUR REPRESENTATIVES (cheers), the men who represented directly the very workman who is going to be robbed of his beer and deprived of all the jollity, festivity, and joviality, and everything which, according to the Hpuse of Lords, makes life worth living (laughter.) THE MISII citunur, that is now a completed transaction, and you will not be suprised to hear that not a single one of the evil consequences then so readily predicted has fallen upon it. In fact, there is not now In Ireland—and it is a statement I make without fear of contradiction—a single pious Protestant adherent of the Protestant Church of Irelarrd who does not look upon Mr. Glad- stone, not as one who reduced his Church into servitude and bondage, but as one who was her great deliverer. From Archbishop, bishops, archdeacons, clergymen, and laity, all in their synods which now govern the Church, freed from the domination of the State, comes the voice, " Never were we so strong, so powerful, so spiritual a community as we are at the present moment." 11r. Gladstame's land policy has taken a longer time to work out ; it has not yet been worked out. Mr. Gladstone himself was somewhat of a late convert to it. Those who knew most about the problem were least consulted. It is still hard,intolerably hard, for any man to do what it is my main duty to do in the House of Commons—to discuss purely Irish questions in a purely English atmosphere. You have to leave off in all your arguments and discussions, however much time may be devoted to them, just where Irishmen in Ireland would begin. Your picture is all out of drawing from the first; it is full of false emphasis, untruthful colouring, delusive lights and shades. There are forty Irish Land Acts on the Statute Book, and these forty Acts are at once the measure an 1 the proof of the INCOMPETENCE OF ONE COUNTEY TO LEGISLATE FOR &NOTHER.

(Cheers.) However, the land policy is moving on, thongh its end is not yet. Land purchase is the last phase of Mr. Gladstone's land policy which began so long ago as 1870. One-half the task is done. Oae-half of the agricultural land in Ireland has already been , transferred, or is now in course of being transferred, ' from the Irish landlord of whom you have heard (laughter)—to the Irish tenant of whom you have also heard. (Cheers.) The other half remains to be done, and it must be done as quickly as possible. The parts of Ireland needing this remedy are the very parts where it has been least applied ; rich landlords in the east who might very well have waited for.their turn, were the very first to avail themselves of the privileges of land purchase, and to get the large bonus which was by the liberality of the British Parliament, placed at the disposal of those Irish landlords who chose to sell on very excellent terms. But in those parts of Ireland in the west, where it was most needed, it has somewhat hung fire, and it is the duty of the Government to see to it that land purchase pr oceeds as quickly as possible. We are told that money is the difficulty, but it is a dEficulty which with care and patience can be overcome; and I think theb Land Bill which I had the honour of introducing the other night—(cheers)—will stand all fair criticism, and will prove to be, if not the lest Irish Land Act, at all events one of the most usefitl. You have to deal with an

===== 0002601_19081205_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====

The Ex:

world. They can be taught the arts of agriculture, and 1 they are being taught them now. Give them more land and increase their holdings,—(cheers)—so that they and their families—or some of them at all events —may there live and thrive. But the question arises WHERE IS THE LAND TO COME FROM? You can only give these people economic holdings by taking away the land which is now used as great cattle farms and grazing farms. There is no other way of doing it. I said the other day in the House of Commons, and I was violently abused for it by many Tory speakers and I have been abused for it ever since in the press. I have amongst other things been called a "quack " because I said the choice in this matter has to be between the people and the cattle. (Cheers.) So it has to be. If you increase the land of these people, you must get the land whereby to increase their hold- ings, and you cannot get that land without taking it from these great cattle farms. The issue is clear : remove the people altogether, or give them more land. If you decide on the latter course, the only land you can give them is that now being employed as large grazing farms. I beg of you to carry this clearly in your minds, and I do not hesitate to brand any man as either ignorant or untruthful who pretends for a single moment that there is any other way of dealing with these poor people than these two ways—emigration or increasing their holdings. Mr. Gladstone's Irish policy was threefold : the Church, the land, and something else. (Laughter.) The Church if a completed transaction ; the land is not yet completed, but it is in course of completion, and I hope before many years are out we shall have seen this great task, if not absolutely completed, almost wholly nom- plated, and all the agricultural land in Ireland trans- ferred under the operation of the Land Acts from the Irish landlord to the Irish tenant. POLICY OF HOME RULE. (Cheers.) On this question everyone in the party must be allowed to speak for himself. rhere are many Tories, I know, who are delighted to hear the Liberal party or any member of the party espouse the cause of Home Rule, because they say to themselves : " This is a fine opportunity for rue; will go to my old chamber of horrors—(laughter)—and I will pullout the Home Rule bogey, and I will trot it through every constituency in the country, and I will get a great many votes." Once again Ireland is to be dragged into party politics and the welfare of Irishmen is to be put on one side, as compared with the opportunity of winning a party victory here at home. It is no concern of mine to con- sider what the effect of declaring yourself a Home Ruler may or may not be. All I know is that I went to Ireland a Home Ruler; and every hour I have remained there has made me more a Home Ruler. (Cheers.) I say that a great country like this has no right whatsoever to continue to inflict upon Irishmen the moral degrada- tion of its present mode of government ;simply because they think that some foolish people might be frightened if she were granted that local autonomy, that local control which she demands. (Hear, hear.) I myself will not be a party to any such reticence or any such deception. (Hear, hear). I sap' that the interests of Ireland and the character of Irishmen and Irishwomen demand that they should hava entrusted to them-in their own local affairs the hard task of self- government. Let the responsibility be theirs, let the task be theirs, and who can doubt that men who exhibit such faculties in every country except their own will rise to the occasion, and will be quite capable of doing what I admit I find very difficult to do— govern Ireland (Cheers). THE LTC FIiSING.BILL.

(Hear, hear). The Licensing Bill was first killed on Tuesday at Lord Lansdowne's House,and then the body, the corpse, of the murdered measure was carried—by a few newspaper boys, I suppose—and laid upon the table of the House of Lords, where it lay for three days, while a debate remarkable, and containing some very powerful speeches, but a debate meaningless and empty of all political force came to its end last night, and then the body, I suppose, was thrown into the street. (Laughter). The only part of the debate which I heard was the concluding speech of our Lord Chancellor. (Cheers). It was a striking scene which I witnessed. The house was crowded with a number of peers some of whom were not very familiar with its precincts (laughter) ; the galleries were filled with ladies, and the whole sight was itself a remarkable one. The Lord Chancellor concluded his speech in words like these : He said he had a great respect for the noble marquis (Lord Lansdowne), but he was quite certain that the transaction which he had just taken part in would not redound to his credit, "because," he said, "the victory that you are new about to record in the lobbies of this house is a victory of a trade over a nation, and of wrong over right." (Cheers.) Those woids were received with the solemnity that they deserved, and a deep silence fell for a moment over the assembly. However, they picked themselves together again, and went into the lobby. (Laughter.) WHO ARE THE MEN
