===== 0002601_19081205_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LICENSING BILL,

Mr. Crosfield said he had heard in London. as no doubt his constituents had heard in Warrington; all sorts of exaggerated nonsense talked about the Government proposals. "The trade " complained about the time limit and yet the man who went through the toil and often sickening anxiety of placing a successful patent upon the market only enjoyed a monopoly of fourteen years. His invention might be a boon to the com- munity at the expiration of that period, but in the case of "the trade," where there was no invention, opponents of the measure would not be satisfied even if the Bill were emended to allow them to retain the monopoly value for twenty-one years before the com- munity resumed possession. In face of the facts he had mentioned, he believed this talk about" robbery" would absolutely defeat itself—in fact it was defeating itself and was bringing more and more of the moderate and unbiased opinion of the country to the side of the Government. (Cheers.) He hoped they in Warrington would stick to the two main root principles in the Bill—the securing of the monopoly value and the reduction in the number of redundant public-houses— and if they fought for themlhe considered their position would be absolutely impregnable. (Cheers.) Continu- ing Mr. Crosfield said that not only in Ireland but also in connection with the tangled and difficult educa- tional problem had Mr. Birrell done much

===== 0002601_19081205_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
SPLENDID PIONEER WORK.

It was not always given to men who were pioneers to see their work brought to full fruition. Coleridge told us that

_ _ "It seems like stories from the land of spirits When any man obtains that which he merits Or merits that which he obtains." But be (Mr. Crosfleld) believed they had good reason to hope that both in a peaceful settlement of the educa- tion problem and in the increased contentment of Ireland and the strengthening of all that was best in the ties which bound Ireland to the Empire, there was no man who would have ground for deeper satisfaction than their guest for the preparatory work he had done. (Cheers.) He was glad that Mr. Birrell, after referring to the Church and land questions, spoke so frankly about the third great issue which seine day would have to be dealt with. When that time came he felt most strongly that the granting of a measure of self-govern- ment to Ireland would be quite as much an act of policy to the advantage of England and to the whole of the Empire as it would undoubtedly be a measure of justice and wisdom to Ireland herself. (Cheers.) The Imperial aspect of Home Rule was often lost sight of, but it must not be forgotten that there u are 20,000,000 of Irishmen beyond the seas and that they necessarily exercised an influence, in every presidential election in the United States, on the imperial politics of that land, as well as of our colonies. Mr.Crosfield asked his hearers not to be led away by that old played-out cry that Home Rule meant Rome Rule. He felt he could speak pretty frankly on the matter, because after all, he had got Irish blood in his veins—(applause)—and he remembered twenty years ago spending a great deal of time to see if there was any justification for that wretched old cry that Protestants in Ireland were being tyrannised and sat upon by their Catholic fellow- countrymen. He could not find a scrap of evidence in support of it. (Cheers.) Moreover, he was strongly of opinion that if in any quarter of Ireland priests did exercise a greater influence than was desirable for the good of the country, there could not possibly be a more effective check on any sal' excess of power than responsible self-government. (Cheers.) In every respect, he felt, as 11‘. Birrell put it in his splendid speech, that the greatest possible boon to Ireland would be the transference to the people of the country of responsibility for their own affairs. It would make them treat politics more seriously than they were sometimes apt to do at present. When that consumma- tion was achieved, and they thought of Mr. Birrell's work in the cause, they must not forget that the great foundation stone of the future edifice of peace in Ireland was laid by Mr. Gladstone. (Cheers). They did well to recall the magnanimity and the heroism with which the great chief fought his battles and faced victory or defeat. "When fortune fled her spoilt and favoured child He stood unbowed beneath the ills upon him piled." Mr. CuosrrruLD concluded : When the time comes for the final settlement and the great handshake across the Channel, and across tha ocean tuo. when at length the rank racial hatreds rooted in the generations are finally laid to rest, then, as of Gladstone and Morley, so of Birrell, it will he remembered to their imperish- able glory how they fought with undaunted courage and untiring energy for a great and noble canse,the ultimate vindication of which will ever more and more as time advances pluck the sorrows from the memory, and raze out the troubles that are written in the brain. (Loud cheers).

MR. HARWOOD, M.P., AND THE LORDS. Mr. G. HARWOOD, M.P., in seconding, said Mr. Birrell combined two qualities very rare in combination —those of being sincere and yet humorous. As a mem- ber of the House of Commons he (Mr. Harwood) thanked him for his burning words, and more especially for having co:pe there bearing the fiery cross. He hoped thcy in Warrington were not as men who were taking off their armour, but as men who were putting it on. The fight was before them ; the gauge of battle had been thrown down, and they were going to take it up. (Cheers.) He did not think that in this tourney the House of Commons was going to be unhorsed.(Cheers.) Birrell quoted from a saying of that great hero Cromwell "to trust in God and keep your powder dry." When he (Mr. Harwood) read the report of the meeting at Lansdowne HOME', and the opposition in the House of Lords on Friday night, there came into his mind another saying of that great man. Watching the hosts of the Scotch descesding the heights of Dunbar he used the expression which put into words what be (Mr. Harwood) felt when he beard that the House of Lords had decided to tear up contemptuously the measure upon which the Commons had spent so much time : " The Lord bath delivered them into our hands." (Laughter and cheers.) The vote was carried with great enthusiasm. Mr. BtrtnELL in reply said :—I am a firm believer in the true Liberal principle " One man, one vote " 1194 am also a firm believer in the other principle." One night, one speech." (Laughter and cheers).

WARRINGTON A REFUTATION OF MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S PROPHECIES.

Mr. KING, M.P., in a brief address, said that some of the happiest years of his life were spent in the good old town of Warrington. He always looked upon it as a brilliant example of the mistakenness of Mr. Chamber- lain's prophecy with regard to tariff reform, because that gentlemen regarded the wire trade as one of the industries which was either dying or dead. He (Mr. King) remembered Warrington for more than 40 years, and every time he visited it be found that they were pulling down their barns and building larger .ones. widening streets, throwing out tramcar lines in all directions, Sm. They were growing out of their old clothes and putting on new ones and all these things were a standing refutation to the gloomy prophecies of Mr. Chamberlain. (Cheers). Con- tinuing Mr. King paid a tribute to the memory of the late revered leader of the party, Sir Henpy Campbell-Bannerman—a man of simple faith and high ideals. Mr. Asquith was worthily following in his footsteps and had gained universal admiration by the bold and consistent manner in which he had maintained and defended the principles of the Licensing Bill in the face of violent opposition—an opposition dis- tinguished more for the strength of the language used than of the arguments employed. (Cheers.) TI ey in Cheshire were proud of themselves and were glad to have a little public recognition of what they had done, and he hoped that what Mr. Birrell had said that night would put heart into the electors, and that they would keep their armour bright, and do as well as they did last time. (Applause).

CHAIRMAN THANKED.

Mr. JAMES DODDS, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Chairman,said he had known Mr. Parker longer than anyone in the hail, even longer than Mrs. Parker had known him. (Laughter.) He was a good man with a clear head, and he was the right man to lead the Liberal party in Warrington to success. He hoped he would be long spared to serve the party and to show what good men were. (Applause). The motion was heartily carried and the meeting closed with the National Anthem.

QUESTIONS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS.

VIII.—If the foreigner would pay a tax on manufactured goods, why would he not pay a tax on raw materials.

Mr. Chamberlain's concrete proposals for "Tariff Reform," made at Glasgow on October 6th, 1903, included a tax of 10 per cent, on manufactured articles, but no tax on raw materials. Tariff Reformers tell us that we should not feel the tax on manufactures, because it would be paid by the foreign exporter. It is also said that as :our imports of manufactures for home consumption amount to £128,000,000 per annum, we should gain by a 10 per cent.tax £12,800,000 of revenue for Old Age Pensions. But if import duties are really paid by foreigners, why stop at manufactures? We import xass,ooo,ooo. of raw materials per annum for home consumption. Ten per cent, on this would yield £18,000,000 for Old Age Pensions. The truth is that Protectionists do not suggest the taxation of raw materials because they are not sure of their own doctrine that the foreigner would pay the British import duties.

L. G. C. L.

===== 0002601_19081205_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Bridge.

was glad to see, from the report of the Mayor's speech at the Caledonian dinner last Monday night, that he is going to specially interest himself in the re-building of Warrington Bridge. For years this proposed bridge has been brought forward from time to time in the Council, and until now nothing has been done in the matter. I am sure the Council feel that they have the support of the Warrington public in this ' good work, and, as the Mayor said, will build a bridge worthy of the town, not less than eighty feet wide. A bridge this size will take about seven to eight hun- dred tons of iron and steel in its construction, and the present present price being £2 to £3 lower than it was eighteen months ago, the saving, if built now, would therefore be very great, roughly £2,000. From my experience,l find that most cities and towns have under consideration the widening or reconstruc- tion of their bridges for the following reasons : Firstly, because now most of them require two lines of trams, and room for vehicles to pass at either side of the lines, including increased motor car traffic. Secondly, when the bridges were constructed engineers and authorities did not take into considera- tion that a bridge has to take all the traffic from the surrounding districts, which is especially true of War- rington Bridge. The main Winwick, Manchester, and Liverpool-roads converge for all the Midiand and Southern Counties atd Wales and vice v.arsa. . .

I have no doubt the engineer could build half the. bridge, say, forty feet in width, then remove the, present bridge, proceeding with the remainder after- wards. II this can be done, traffic would not be impeded-

whilst the Bridge was under construction. Wishing the Mayor and the Bridge Committee every success in their undertaking,—Yours truly, EDWIN WOODS.

Rock Villa, Latchford, Cheshire. 4th December, 1908.

MAYORAL "AT HOME.-

The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Forshaw) held an " At Home " at the Town Hall on Thursday afternoon, and a highly successful function it proved. From three to five o'clock there was a continuous stream of visitors, and the number of ladies and gentlemen who paid their respects to the chief magistrate and his wife must have numbered close - upon 1,000. The entrance hall had been very taste- fully decorated with plants and flowers by Mr. R. Whitfield, head gardener, and presented a bright and attractive appearance. The visitors were received in the large committee room. The Mayoress was wearing a handsome gown of blue poplin-de-chine, the corsage trimmed with a handsome embroidery, made in gold and two shades of blue to tone with the gown, filled in with ecr net and velours chiffon. The gown was made b Messrs. W. Hodgkinson, Ltd. Light refreshments were served in the Council Chamber, Mrs. Fowls having charge of the catering. In the entrance hall Mr. R. H. Pearson's b6d played the following choice programme in a manner which afforded great pleasure Overture, " Bohemian Girl " Baiff. Intermezzo Macbeth,. Waltz Wein Weih and Gesang Strauss. REV. J. F. MATTHEWS.

Important Call to Sheffield Accepted.

The Bev. J. F. Matthews, pastor of the Baptist Church, Latchford, has accepted the .pastorate of the Glossop-road Baptist Church, Sheffield, which has been vacant since January. At a meeting of the, congregation held on Monday evening a unanimcus- invitation was extended to Mr. Matthews to take up the work. Mr. Matthews, who was present at the . . „ . . . .

meeting; intimated his acceptance of the invitation. Despite his comparative youth—he is just 42 —Mr. Matthews goes to Sheffield with a record of eighteen years' good work in the ministry. It was not at first anticipated that Mr. Matthews should become the power in Free Church life that he undoubtedly is • he was originally intended for the Anglican Church'. Mr. Matthews' sympathies, however, were captured by the Baptist creed. He absorbed the elements of his theology beneath, the pulpit of Westbourne Park Church, London, with the revered Dr. Clifford as his mentor. He continued the process of absorption durin"g four years' study at , the Midland Baptist and University Colleges, Notting- ham, from 1835 to 1890, taking first place for Greek in his last year. His first pastorate, in which he settled in 1390, was at Auilem, in Cheshire. There he remained seven years, and at the end of that period he responded to a call from the congregation at Latchford. That was, eleven years ago, and his stay at Latchford has been characterised by ardent and fruitful effort in the cause lof Christianity and social reform Mr. Matthews has. ever been to the fore in Free Church and moral move- ments in Warrington, and while he is a keen contro- versialist, his methods are such as to command the- respect even of those most strongly opposed to binsa. and everyon3 recognizes his earnestness and sincerity in any cause which he espouses. Daring the time that he acted as Secretary of the Warrington Free. Church Council he threw himself into the work with commendable energy. Mr. Matthews' political views are entirely Socialistic. Besides being a member of the London Fabian Society he has while in Watrington identified himself wit the Independent Labour Party, and in connection witd the latter organization he conducted a very successful class for embryo speakers, and another class for " the dissemination of economic truths."

While congratulating Mt:. Matthews on his selection for such an inflnontial pastorate his friends in Nyarrington— and they are legion— will,sincerely regret his departure from our midst, and he will leave the twin with the heartiest good wishes for his future prosperity. As the " SheaAd Daily Independent" well says : " The hands of the religious and social forces in the city will be considerably strengthened by the acquisition of a man of such a righteous- aggressiveness as Mr. Matthews."
